Renn knotted the chain into a loop at the near end with fingers that shook and then did not. The steward watched him do it. She did not stop him, and Sol understood why with a small cold turn of the stomach: her house needed the plate balanced as badly as they did.

"The drop is four breaths now," Sol said. "I call it. We pull on the drop and we let go on the swell. Nobody's hands stay on wet rope when the line rises."

"Yours first, or mine?"

"Both. Side by side. You have the weight, I have the count."

They knelt at the arch with the rope between them, and Sol set her palms on it and closed her eyes. The line breathed under the black water. Down, and down, and down, and she felt the last of the drop like a stair under her foot. "Now."

They pulled. The chain slid off the landing and hissed over the surface in a long grey arc, and at the edge of her hearing the note began to climb. "Off." Their hands opened. The chain lay a third of the way across, and the seam beneath it brightened, and a filament of charge crawled up the wall and vanished.

"Down. Now." Pull. "Off." Pull. Off. The chain came on like a slow animal, and Renn at the far end counted under his breath in a voice that had lost all its dryness, and Kael's breathing beside her was steady as a lock turning.

On the fifth pull, the seam did not wait.

It rose on the seventh breath, not the eighth, and the swell came up the wet rope faster than any voice could have called it. Sol's palms were flat on the rope. The charge reached her before it reached Kael, because his hands were a length farther back on rope that had stayed dry. It was cold, a white frost that arrived in her wrists and burned outward, and she heard herself make a small flat sound as her fingers locked.

"Let go," Kael said. "Sol. Let go."

She could not. The rope was the only thing between the chain and the water, and her hands had decided for her. Then the swell fell, all at once, and her fingers opened by themselves, and she was on her knees with her palms turned up in the witch-light. The skin was white, then red, then rising in long shining welts from the heel of each hand to the root of every finger.

Kael did not touch her. He took the rope from the place where her hands had lain and looked at her.

"Can I finish it?"

"Yes." It came out as a breath. "I'll call it. I don't need hands to count."

He hauled the last of the rope alone, on her word, drop by drop, and the chain came up onto the dry floor of the alcove in a wet grey coil. Together they lifted it, or Kael lifted it and Sol guided him, her hands held out from her body as if they were not hers. Coil by coil it went onto the pedestal, over the pale ring, and Kael pressed the last link flat.

For a long moment the water did nothing. Then the seam dimmed by degrees. The swell that had come at seven, at eight, came at nine, then ten. It stopped at ten, and held.

"Ten," said Sol, staring at the line. "Not eleven. Near enough." Her voice shook. "It's gone back to sleep. It hasn't decided who owes it. Not yet."

Kael knelt in front of her. He tore a strip from the hem of his shirt, and then another, and looked up. "I'd like to wrap them."

"Do it."

He did it slowly, and she watched his face and not her hands. He did not say he had warned her. He said, very quietly, "I hate that it was you," and she said, "I know. Thank you for saying it," and neither of them said anything else.

They crossed on the drop, at ten. Kael carried the box against his chest, and Sol walked beside him counting for them both, and this time the black water let them through. At the foot of the stair the three retainers had swung their staves across the way up. The steward stood behind them with the lantern in her hand.

"You have kept my house's lamps burning, Auditor," she said. "I am grateful."

"We did it for the households," Sol said.

"So did we." The steward's eyes rested on the box. "For three years, so did we."

The tallest retainer took a step forward, staff angled. Kael did not look at him. He looked at Sol, and she nodded once, and they filled their lungs together.

"Joren!"

"Mara!"

Either voice would have brought the wardens; that was the order Kael had given at the hatch, with Sol beside him so they would hear it in both. Neither of them had used it until now. A fight on the landing of a flooded gallery would have put one of them in the water, and they had chosen to make the call together or not at all. Boots hit the spiral above almost before the echo did, many of them and quick, and a hard voice called down the stair: "Coming, Auditor!"

The steward raised two fingers. The staves lowered, and the retainers drew back against the wall like a tide going out.

"The hearing is on the twenty-fourth," she said mildly, as they passed her. "In front of the Council. It will be decided there which of us the Reach can afford."

Varel rose from the bottom step and fell in behind them without a word. He walked as if the stair might be taken away.

They came up into the grey of the ropewalk gallery as the first bell began. Joren's scarred hand took the box from Kael, then gave it back, and Mara took one look at Sol's bound hands and said nothing and put her own cloak around her shoulders. Renn set the box on the low bench under the high window. From his satchel he took a strip of Council wax and warmed it, and laid it across the seam of the lid beside the near-black wax that lay there unbroken. The strip took his commission seal. Beside it he cut the hour and the date with the point of his stylus, and his hand did not shake at all.

"Twenty-three Sunspire," he said. "First bell. Unbroken."

Through the high window the Lower Reach was waking under lamps that had never gone out. Forty households, fifty. Every one of them had lived three years by the light of a debt no one had told them they owed. Sol stood with her ruined hands held out from her sides and looked down at Ansa's window over the tar-shop, where a lamp burned steady and yellow.

"What's it for?" Varel said behind her. He was not looking at the box. He was looking at the lamps. "All that charge. Three years. What was it for?"

Nobody answered him. The box lay on the bench between them, sealed, stamped, twelve pounds near enough. It was hers to carry into the Council chamber and nobody's to open before then.

## Chapter 16

The Council chamber had been scrubbed overnight, and it still smelled of the ropewalk. Kael noticed it as he crossed the marble at ninth bell: tar and wet hemp clinging to Renn's coat and to the wardens at the door and, faintly, to the box on the clerk's table, which sat between two seals with nothing else near it.

Sol walked beside him. Not behind, not ahead. Her hands hung at her sides in fresh linen, the healer's grey salve showing at the edges of the wrappings, and every eye in the tiered gallery went to them and then away, as if looking were an intrusion. She had refused the sling. She had refused the chair. She had said, that morning, in the voice she used for measurements, that she would stand for this or not attend.

He had said, "Then we stand." He had not said the other thing. He had counted the days since the ropewalk instead, and the number was one.

Lord Malrik Thorne came in on his grandson's arm, in deep grey, the silver clasp at his throat, white hair combed flat and spine straight. Behind the Thorne bench, among the retainers in violet, sat a small upright woman with a copper clasp. She met Kael's eyes across the chamber and inclined her head. It was a courtesy that kept score.

Eight houses had pressed their signatures into the petition on the Chancellor's dais. Eight of twelve. Under the Accord's own procedure that number obliged a vote within the same session as the hearing, and the whole chamber knew it. The Chancellor's gavel struck once. Her voice was thin with age and reached every tier.

"The Council convenes on the petition of House Thorne for administration of the ward-trade reform. The petition states that the lead auditor's report is forthcoming. Auditor Ashworth."

Kael stood. The signet ring was cold against his knuckle. "There is no report, Chancellor."

A murmur climbed the tiers.

"I say so now, so that the Council does not hear it first from another mouth. There is no report. There is a finding, and it is short." He turned his palm toward the table. "Under the ropewalk gallery in the Lower Reach, in a sublevel the register lists as contested, the Vault's main trunk line has been tapped for three years and metered against a scale. The ledger that records it is in two hands. One belongs to a clerk who is in this chamber under Council protection. Every page is copied and stamped by Renn of the Council survey commission, with the hour and the date. The tap's lock plate bears the Thorne knot."

He did not look at the Thorne bench. He felt it, the way a man feels an open door behind him.

Malrik Thorne rose without help. "May the house answer?"

"The house may answer."

"My house kept the Lower Reach lit for three years." The old man's voice was dry and even and carried no anger at all. "The Accord's works office forgot that trunk line existed the day the Vault was sealed. Forty households would have gone dark in the first winter. They did not, and the reason is on the record the auditor has just read into it. Thorne does not apologize for lamps." He let the silence gather. "Nor does the house fear the box on that table. It bears our wax. Open it, Chancellor, and let the Council read what we have kept safe."

Sol stepped forward. She had not been called. Kael did not reach for her, because she was not his to reach for. "Chancellor. Isolde Vane, accredited ward-taker, joint petitioner. I ask leave to add to the finding."

"Granted."

She held up her wrapped hands, not as an appeal but as evidence. "The line under that sublevel is balanced this morning by a chain, not by Thorne's iron. It is the Council surveyor's own measuring chain, twelve pounds near enough, lying on a plate. My lord is right that forty households are lit. He is wrong that they are safe. They are lit until the chain slips, and the Council's chain is standing in for a Thorne ingot."

A single laugh broke in the upper tier and died. The woman with the copper clasp did not move.

The Chancellor waited until the chamber had finished breathing. "The Council does not vote on a claim while an instrument sealed in the claimant's own wax lies unopened in its custody. It has never done so. It will not begin today." The gavel came down, not hard. "The vote is held. The instrument will be opened in this chamber before the full Council at ninth bell on the twenty-eighth, its seals examined by the Council's clerks. Until then the sublevel stays under Council guard, the chain stays where it lies, and the Ashworth wardens keep the ropewalk floor." She looked down at the old man. "The petitioner has said the house does not fear the box."

"It does not," said Malrik Thorne. He bowed exactly as far as courtesy required and not a hair farther, and his grandson took his elbow.

Kael watched them go and thought: he wanted it opened. He has wanted it opened for three years. He could not have said why that frightened him more than a refusal would have.

---

They did not speak of it until the lamps were lit.

Supper sat untouched on the table. Sol had taken the chair by the window, her wrapped hands lying in her lap like parcels she was minding for a friend.

"The salve," she said. "The healer gave it to you at the door. Is it still in your coat?"

"Left pocket." He was at the washstand with his back to her. He heard her rise, and then the small sounds of a person refusing help: the wrists working the coat down off its hook, the patient shuffle of linen against wool. He thought she was allowed that. He was drying his hands when the sounds stopped.

A scrap of vellum lay on the floor between her boots. Cream, stiff, with a seal pressed at its foot in wax the color of a moonless night. She did not pick it up. She could not have, and she did not ask him to. She looked at it, and then she looked at him, and he knew before she spoke exactly which day it was dated.

"Read it to me," she said.

He crossed the room. He did not pick it up either. "Entry pass. The Precedent Archive of House Thorne, lower gallery, western face. Twenty-three Sunspire. Bearer, Kael Ashworth, Auditor. Authority, the seal of House Thorne."

"Three strands." Her eyes were on the wax. "A drop, a feather, a flame. I could draw it blind." Her voice was level. "The twenty-third. The day we came up out of the ropewalk. You said you would be at the clerk's desk filing Renn's sheaves, and I should sit with the healer and let my hands rest."

"Yes."

"You didn't mention the archive."

"No."

The lamp hissed. In the spire a bell marked the half hour. She stood with the coat still hanging from her wrists like a shed skin, and she said the sentence he had known in a low place, since the gallery, he would one day be given.

"I'm not asking you to tell me everything." She said it evenly. "I'm asking you to stop deciding what I don't need to know."

He did not look away. On the stair he had prepared a defense: the healer, the hands, the day's exhaustion, the fact that he had told her the truth about the clerk's desk, because he had gone there, afterward, for an hour. It was a good defense. It read beautifully. He left it where it lay.

"You're right," he said. "I decided. There is no version of that day where I didn't."

She waited. She had always known how to wait him out.

"I have reasons. I'll give them to you if you want them. None of them are excuses."

"Give them."

"The steward's speech. It went in, Sol. I wanted to know whether it was true, and I did not want you standing beside me when I found out that it was." He drew a breath. "And I had watched the rope take your hands. The idea of you inside a building Thorne owns, hours later, was more than I could carry. So I carried the visit instead, alone, and called it paperwork." He stopped. "That's the whole of it. It isn't enough."

"No," she said. "It isn't." But she had not moved to leave. "What was in the archive?"

He told her. He told it the way Renn told a page, in order, with nothing added. A keeper named Cormac Vrell had met him at the gate, given him the pass, and walked him to the shelves, and had not left him alone with them for a minute. The ledgers were Thorne's own ward-service records, three generations of them, by name and by year. The dead had ink beside them. There were more names than he could read in an afternoon.

"It's real," he said. "The grievance is real, and the Accord's records hold almost none of it. I read nothing about the Deed. I went for Thorne's own history and I read only Thorne's own history."

She was quiet for a long moment. "Is there anything else?"

He had been waiting for the question since the hearing, and he was ready. "One thing. On the fourth tier there's a shelf of contracts filed by house. Vane. I saw a spine with your mother's name on it." He kept his voice steady. "I did not take it down. It isn't mine to open."

Sol's face did not change, but her breath left her all at once. She looked at the pass. When she spoke her voice had dropped almost to nothing.

"Thank you for that part."

"It doesn't fix it."

"No." She lifted her wrists, and he understood, and took the coat from them and hung it on its hook. She watched him do it. "Tomorrow, first bell, we go back. Together. I read what I choose to read, and you stand where I can see you. And the next pass you are handed, you show me before you use it. Not after."

"Before."

"Four days to the twenty-eighth, Kael. I can't do this with you deciding which rooms I'm allowed to be in."

"The pass will take a second name," he said. "Vrell told me at the gate. Any bearer may add one."

She nodded once. She sat back down in the chair by the window, and after a moment he found the healer's jar in the coat where he had left it and knelt in front of her, and she held out her hands.

He worked the salve into the edges of the linen without unwrapping it, and neither of them said anything. The lamp burned. On the floor between them the pass lay where it had fallen, its near-black wax gone glossy in the light.

"Why would they give it to you?" Sol asked at last, watching the vellum and not him. "Thorne can refuse an auditor anything it likes. It opened its private archive to you and asked no question at the gate."

He had been asking himself that since the stair, and he still had no answer. He told her so, and it was true.

He rose to draw the shutter. On the rope bridge between the spires a hooded figure stood in the last of the dusk, not hiding, a copper clasp catching the light at its throat, and it was looking at their window. It did not move when he saw it. It let him see it. Then it turned and walked away across the swaying boards.

## Chapter 17

There were more of them at the gate than there had been. Sol counted six retainers in violet along the stair cut into the western face, one at each landing, and she counted them the way her mother had taught her to count anything that might matter: once for the number, once for what it cost them to stand so still.

"When I came there were two," Kael said, low, beside her.

"Then Lord Malrik has been busy."

The gate itself was a plain arch of dark stone set into the cliff with a lamp on either side, and beneath the lamps a table, a ledger, and a man in grey scholar's robes with violet piping at the cuffs. He rose as they approached. He was narrow and pale, his dark hair drawn back, with the courteous stillness of a person who spends his days among things that do not hurry.

"Auditor Ashworth. And Isolde Vane." He bowed to each of them exactly the same depth. "Cormac Vrell, keeper of the Precedent Archive. The pass admits a second bearer. Will the lady sign?"

His eyes went to her hands and away again at once, and she gave him credit for both. "I can't hold a pen," Sol said. "I'm told it will be a few days."

"Then permit me." He wrote her name in the ledger himself, in a small square hand, and turned the book so she could see it. *Isolde Vane, bearer, second.* "Your hands were spoken of in the city," he said quietly. "The house is sorry for the cost."

He said it without weight, as a matter of record, and she could not find the edge of it. That was the worst thing about Thorne. It had no edges she could find.

The archive lay behind the arch: one long vaulted chamber cut into the rock, shelves climbing its walls in tiers reached by narrow iron ladders, oil lamps set at even intervals, and at the far end a window with nothing behind it but air and the sheer drop to the Reach floor. It smelled of vellum and cold iron. Sol had spent years in archives, and this was the first one that felt as though it were listening.

"Two days ago the auditor read the ward-service ledgers," Vrell said. "The lady may wish to see what he saw."

"I do." She looked at Kael. "Stand where I can see you."

He moved to the end of the reading table and stayed there.

Vrell set the first volume open on the table and stood back. Kael turned the pages at her word, and she read.

It was as he had said. Name after name in cramped clerks' hands, year by year, three generations of a house that had stood in the gap of the old trade and let it take what it took. A warden of the Lower Spine, dead at thirty-one, the cost entered in the same neat column as a grain delivery. Two brothers, one after the other, seven years apart. A girl of fifteen, contracted to the eastern line, with a note in the margin: *did not come home.* Beside each death a dot of red-brown ink had dried in the same place on the page, as if one hand had touched every entry with the same finger.

"It goes on for two more volumes," Kael said quietly.

"I know." Her throat was tight. "I don't need the other two. I believe it." She meant it, and it did not change what she had come to say. "Keeper. Thorne's petition claims the reform mandate by right of this. Is that the argument the house will make on the twenty-eighth?"

"The house will make whatever argument its lord chooses." Vrell's voice was soft. "I keep the records. I do not draft the petitions. But I was asked to say one thing, if the auditors came." He folded his hands. "Lord Malrik asks that you read all of it before the Council opens the box, so that you do not oppose him in ignorance of what the house has paid. He says he would rather be opposed by a person who has read it than admired by one who has not."

Sol looked at Kael. His face gave her nothing, and then it gave her exactly what she needed: that he had heard the trap in the courtesy and the truth in the courtesy in the same breath, and that he was going to let her say which of them weighed more.

"Thank the lord for me," she said. "I will read every page. And on the twenty-eighth I will oppose him anyway, and I would like it recorded that both of those things are true."

"It is recorded," said Vrell. The corner of his mouth moved and stilled.

She had promised herself she would not ask. She asked.

"The fourth tier. The contracts filed under Vane."

Vrell did not blink. "The auditor did not take it down."

"I know. He told me." She turned her head. "Will you?"

"Yes."

"Bring it to the table. Then open it. I can't turn the pages, and I don't want anyone else's hands on it."

He climbed the iron ladder, and she watched every rung of it. He came down with a slim volume in cracked green leather, a Vane spine, and set it beneath the lamp. He looked at her. She nodded. He opened it, and it fell open the way such books do, to the page most often read.

The contract was in a clerk's fair hand, the ink gone brown. *Ward-taker, House Vane.* The year of her mother's binding. A term of service: *until dissolution of the trade or death of the bearer.* Sol had known that. She had known the shape of it from a hundred conversations that stopped when she walked into the room. What she had not known was the schedule at the foot of the page, added in a quicker hand, in ink that had not faded so evenly. A list headed *Dependents*, and beneath it a single line.

*Isolde, daughter, age 8.*

Her mother's hand. She would have known it anywhere. She had grown up reading that small forward-leaning script on the backs of receipts and along the edges of maps. It had never occurred to her that her name might be here. It had never occurred to her that her mother had stopped, in a Thorne clerk's office, in the middle of signing away her years, to make certain the record showed there was a child.

Sol stood very still. The linen on her hands was white against the dark wood. Under it the welts had begun to knit; the healer had said so that morning, changing the grey salve, the skin closing at the fingertips first, a fist by the end of the week if she did not force one before then. She had forced one twice anyway, in the grey before first bell, and paid for it both times. Now her fingers curled inside the wrappings toward the name on the page, wanting to touch it, and could not. Kael did not move or speak, and he did not turn the page until she said, "Turn it."

The next page held the counter-signature. A steward's hand, flat and firm: *Received for the works of the Lower Spine, House Thorne.* Beside it the knot, three strands, in near-black wax. Her mother had signed on Thorne's line. Thorne had held the other end of the same rope.

"They were on the same line," Sol said. She had not meant to say it aloud. "Her and Thorne's wards. The same system."

"Yes," said Vrell. "I believe that is why the pass was extended."

She looked at him. He met her eyes with the patience of a man who had already lost the argument and did not mind. "Lord Malrik does not think the house and the Vanes were enemies," he said. "He thinks the trade made them neighbors. He would like you to have the page."

It was a gift and a lever at once, and she could not put it down. "Kael. Ask the keeper for a copy under the archive's seal. The whole page, and the one after."

"It will be sent to the lady by noon," said Vrell.

They read for two more hours. She read the second volume and the third, and Kael turned the pages, and no one in the room spoke unless a page required it. At the end she asked the keeper one more question, because it had been sitting in her since the gate.

"Why are there six retainers on the stair?"

"The house has petitioned the Council for a custodial role over its own precedents." Vrell said it as he had said everything, evenly. "Until the Council rules, the house keeps watch on what is its own, and on those who use it."

"You are watching us."

"The house is watching everyone, Lady Vane. It learned the habit from being watched."

They went up together. The stair cut into the western face was long and the wind came at it sideways, and Sol climbed with her hands held close and did not let Kael take her elbow. She did let him climb one step below her, where she could see him. At the third landing she stopped.

"From here," she said. "No more passes I haven't seen. No more days I hear about after. I'm not asking for a signature on a Council roll. I'm asking for that."

"From here," Kael said. It was not a speech, and she could see he knew that. "Together. Before."

She set the back of her wrist against his chest, briefly, the way she had once set her whole hand, and took it away. It was all the touch her hands would allow, and it was enough.

A boy in Accord grey waited at the top of the stair with a folded card. He held it out to Kael, and then, after a glance at Sol's hands, he held it out to the space between them instead. Violet paper. Near-black wax. The knot. Kael broke the seal and read it and passed it to her, because she could not have held it otherwise, and she read it over his arm.

*Lord Malrik Thorne requests the company of Auditor Kael Ashworth and Isolde Vane at supper tomorrow, at the seventh bell, in the house at the top of the Reach. He asks that neither come alone.*

Sol read the last line twice. The first card, a week ago, had been addressed to Kael alone, and he had refused it. This one carried both their names.

## Chapter 18

The house at the top of the Reach had no gate. It had a stair, wide and shallow, with a lamp under a violet glass shade at every fourth step and two retainers at the head of it who bowed to Sol before they bowed to him. Kael noted the order. He noted everything at Thorne now, the way he would have noted a column in a ledger that a careful hand had made to look like nothing.

Sol climbed beside him with her hands held close to her ribs. The healer had changed the linen that morning to a finer weave, and she could curl her fingers as far as the second knuckle, though not press them against anything. She had told him so over breakfast in the level voice she kept for figures. He had answered in kind, "Thank you for telling me," and she had looked at him a moment as if checking a sum, and let it stand.

Two pages lay folded in her coat pocket under the archive's seal, delivered by noon yesterday, as promised. She had shown him the envelope and not the pages. That was hers. He was learning its outline.

A small upright woman with a copper clasp met them in the hall and led them through without a word. The panelling was dark, the lamp brackets copper, the walls bare. Kael had expected portraits and found none, and understood that a house which kept its dead in ledgers had no need to hang them.

The dining room held a round table laid for four, and one wall that was all window: the long drop to the Reach floor, the lamps coming on tier by tier below like an account being totted up. Lord Malrik Thorne stood by his chair without help. At home he wore the same deep grey, the same plain silver at his throat, the same straight spine, and he was smaller than Kael had let himself remember.

"Auditor. Lady Vane." He inclined his head to each, and again the depth was the same. "You came together. I asked it, and did not expect to be obeyed."

"Neither of us was going to come alone," Sol said.

"No. I see that." The old face approved of the answer and did not trouble to hide it. "My grandson, Aldous."

The young man beside him was perhaps twenty-five, with his grandfather's height and none of his ease. He drew out Sol's chair, looked stricken at having touched it, and stepped back. She said, "Thank you," and sat, and he seemed to breathe again.

There was no cutlery at her place. There was a cup of broth with two wide handles, bread already torn, cheese cut in squares, a pear halved and cored. Kael saw her see it.

"The house is attentive," she said.

"The house has had practice." Malrik lowered himself into his chair. "In the last year of the trade our infirmary wing fed forty people who could not hold a spoon. It became a habit of the kitchen. Habits outlive their reasons."

Sol did not smile. She lifted the cup between both palms, slowly, her wrists doing the work, and drank, and set it down without losing a drop. Kael kept his hands flat on the cloth. "The cheese," he said, low. "On the bread, or as it is?"

"On the bread." She waited a breath. "Only that."

He laid four squares on the torn bread and took his hand away. It was a very small thing, and he felt the whole table watching him do it correctly.

"Vrell tells me you read every page," Malrik said.

"I read every page."

"And found nothing to admire."

"I found a great deal I believe. It isn't the same." She held his eyes. "There were red-brown dots beside the dead. Always in the same place on the page."

Malrik looked at his own right hand, lying on the cloth, the forefinger stained faintly at the tip. "Oak gall and rust. I make the ink myself. Every year at the turn of the season I go down to the archive and touch each name, so that the clerk's column does not become the whole record." He said it the way a man reports the weather. "It takes four days now."

Aldous looked at his plate. Kael understood that the boy had never once been allowed to skip the descent.

"The two pages," Sol said. "My mother's contract. I want to say one thing about them."

"They were not put on account," said Malrik. "If that is what you wish to know."

"It is." She drew a breath. "Then I will say thank you here, once, and it won't be filed."

"It will not be filed."

The steward stood behind the old man's chair and did not move. Kael thought she was counting.

They ate for a quarter of an hour. It was not a bad meal, and Kael tasted very little of it. The talk went where such talk goes: the drought on the eastern terraces, the price of hemp, whether Renn would be made a full commissioner. Malrik was courteous and well informed and, once, unexpectedly funny about the Chancellor's habit of ruling in the gap between two other people's sentences. Then he set down his cup.

"I will not insult you with a long approach, Auditor."

"I would rather you didn't."

"Your father's name stood behind the Accord's instruments, and the Reach believed them because it was his. Thorne asks the same of the sitting auditor. Not a false word. A true one, spoken by the man the Council trusts, before the Council opens the box on the twenty-eighth."

The steward stepped forward and laid a folded slip beside Kael's plate. He did not open it.

"What true word?"

"That for three years House Thorne kept the Lower Reach lit at its own cost. That the tap was a stewardship."

"The ledger calls it a tap on a trunk line that belonged to the Vault. It calls the man who kept the meter a prisoner."

"The clerk was kept," said Malrik. "I am told he was warm. I am not proud of the locked door, and I would lock it again." He said this evenly too, with no heat in it at all. "You have found the tap. You have not yet asked what happens when it is closed."

Kael had. He had asked it in the gallery with the water at his knees, and had not liked the arithmetic. "The trunk line surges back up the tap."

"Unless it is shut in order. There is an order. My smiths took three years to learn it and set it down in one hand, and one person holds the page." Malrik folded his hands. "The Council's chain is a brave thing, Auditor. It is twelve pounds of survey iron doing the work of a Thorne ingot, and it will slip, and when it slips forty households go dark with the water coming up under them. When the Council opens the box it will argue for weeks. The lamps must be kept lit through the arguing. I would prefer that it be a house that knows how to shut the tap. I would prefer that you were the one to say so."

The trap, if it was one, was built entirely of true things. Kael felt the pull of it low and strong, like the charge in the gallery on the swell: certify the sentence, take the order, retire the tap in the dark before it could drown anyone, and carry the cost of the certifying alone, as he had carried the archive and the coat and the ledger. It would be clean, and it might even be right. It was the very shape of the thing Sol had named in the lamplight two nights ago, made large enough to walk into.

"And if I say the opposite?" he asked.

"Then I say it myself, in the same chamber, and the Council chooses between a house it distrusts and forty households in the dark." Malrik's voice did not change. "I have counted the votes. I would rather not win that way. I will if I must."

Kael looked at Sol. Not to ask permission. To see her. She had set both palms flat against the cloth and was watching the folded slip, not the old man, and her face was doing the sum he knew it was doing.

"I'm not going to answer that," he told Malrik. "Not at this table, and not alone. Whatever I say to the Council, I will say after I have spoken to her."

"That is your right."

"Read us the sentence," said Sol.

Kael unfolded the slip and read it aloud, because her hands could not have held it. "House Thorne kept the Lower Reach lit for three years, at its own cost, in good faith and in the public interest, and stands ready to make the trunk line safe." He lowered it. "In good faith is a finding. I can't certify a finding I haven't made."

"Then strike the words," Malrik said at once. "Keep the lamps. Keep the last clause." He turned to Sol. "Lady Vane, the offer is to the auditor, but it is to you as well. I would sooner be opposed by both of you than tolerated by either."

"You say things twice, my lord, in different words, until they sound like law." Her voice was steady. "I heard it at the archive. I'll hear it again on the twenty-eighth."

Aldous coughed into his hand, and might have been hiding a smile.

"The house files its statement with the Chancellor's clerk at the seventh bell tomorrow," Malrik said. "It will say one of two things. It will say that Thorne offers the shutting order to the Council in good faith, with the sitting auditor's word behind it. Or it will say that Thorne offers it against the auditor's silence, and the Council may draw its own conclusions from the difference. I would like to know which before I set my seal to it. Give me your answer by the sixth bell, before the day's first session."

"You'll have it," Kael said. He did not say which.

Aldous saw them down. The retainers at the head of the stair bowed to Sol first again, and she inclined her head to each, and Kael found that he minded the courtesy less than he had on the way up. At the third lamp from the bottom she stopped, and he stopped with her.

"I wanted to say yes," he said. "At the table. I'd have said it alone and told you after, and it would have been a good sentence in a clean ledger. I want you to have that from me now, not later."

She looked at him for a long moment. "Thank you for saying it before I asked."

"It doesn't change what he's holding."

"No. He's right about the chain." She turned her wrapped hands over in the violet light and studied them as if they were a document. "I set it on that plate. It won't stand forever, and I knew that when I did it."

"I know."

They went on down. Behind and above them the house put out its lamps one violet shade at a time, until only the dining room window still burned, where an old man sat on alone beside a cold cup, waiting for a bell.

## Chapter 19

They did not sleep. They argued instead, properly, at the table by the window with the lamp turned low and the folded slip lying between them where neither of them would have to touch it.

"The case for yes," Sol said. "Say it plainly."

"The chain is failing." Kael did not soften it. "Not tonight. But it's iron in wet air, standing in for a Thorne ingot, and the tap will have to be shut in the right order before it fails for good. If Malrik does it, forty households never learn how close it came. What I'd pay for that is a sentence in a hearing."

"And the case for no."

"He wants the auditor to tell the Reach that a house which locked a man in a cellar was its steward. Said in that chamber, it stays said." He turned the slip a quarter turn with one finger. "And he wants it said before the box is opened. Before anyone has read what's inside. I'd be vouching for a house whose main paper I haven't seen."

"That's the one."

"That's the one," Kael agreed. He looked at the window, at the lamps of the lower tiers, and she watched a sentence arrive on his face and be put down unspoken. "I'll go and look at the chain, and then I'll..." He stopped. "No. Sol, will you come with me? I want to see what it's doing before I answer him."

She had learned to count small things. "Yes," she said, and took her coat down from its hook by the wrists, and he held it open, and she let him.

The Lower Reach in the small hours smelled of tar and wet hemp. Ansa's window over the tar shop was lit, and a rope creaked on a bridge. The ropewalk gallery ran away from them into the dark like a loom abandoned mid-thread, and at its far end two of Ashworth grey stood by the hatch beside two of the Council's guards, and none of the four looked as though they had slept either. Mara nodded to Sol. Joren, the scar white across the back of his hand, said, "My lord. My lady," and then, "Renn's been down the stair twice since dusk. He won't say why."

"He counts," Sol said. "It's what he has."

Renn sat at a trestle inside the hatch office with a lamp, a ledger, and a board of ten brass pegs, and he came awake so fast the pegs jumped in their holes. "Auditor. Lady Vane." He said it as though he had been caught out. "I have the count."

"Which count?" Kael asked.

"Both." He turned the ledger toward them. Two columns in his cramped hand: the cycle in breaths, the chain in links. "I take the links through a glass from the near edge of the gallery, on the drop, at every change of guard. Ten pins, one hundred links, the plumb. I laid it straight on the plate so the count would be honest. The cycle I take from the landing." He looked at Sol and away. "I can't feel it as you do. I count the wall lamp."

"Let me," Sol said.

They went down the spiral stair, the dry part, to the second landing where the walls began to sweat. Kael went one step below her, where she could see him, and did not offer his elbow. The stone was cold enough to bite through the linen. She stopped on the landing and shut her eyes.

The line came up through the wall the way it always did, less a sound than a change in the weight of the air. A rise. A held breath. The discharge up the walls, and the drop. She counted it against her own pulse, which ran too fast, and again against the slow tick of water below, which did not.

"Ten," she said. "Steady. Ten, ten, ten." She opened her eyes. "It hasn't moved."

"It has held since I laid the chain," Renn said behind them, low. "I counted last at the tenth bell."

"Then he said it would slip." Kael's voice was level. "How fast?"

Renn had done the sum. She could tell by how he answered without touching the ledger. "Iron in a wet gallery loses weight at the surface. A chain that size, an ounce a week, perhaps two. The plate forgives a few ounces before the cycle notices. It falls to nine by the middle of Cinderveil and to eight by the end of it, if nothing is done." He swallowed. "And eight is where it surges."

"Weeks," Sol said. "Not days."

"Weeks. It's a real problem, my lady. It isn't an emergency yet."

She stood a moment longer with her back against the wet stone. Malrik had told them the truth and dressed it in the size of an emergency. There would be weeks. The Council would argue through them, and the weeks would run out on a day Thorne had not troubled to mention.

"He's right about the chain," she said. "He's wrong about the hour."

"Both," said Kael.

They climbed back up into the lamplight. On the bench Renn's tally board stood open, its ten pegs ranked in their holes, and Sol, who had not tried to close a hand around anything since the ropewalk, put out her right hand and set thumb and forefinger against the nearest peg. Her fingers screamed inside the linen. She held on for a count of four, moved the peg one hole to the left, and let it go. It rocked once and was still. Nobody said a word. Mara, sharpening her blade at the far end of the bench, studied the whetstone with great attention.

"Four," Renn said quietly, and wrote it in the margin. He did not write what it was a count of.

Kael drafted the reply on the back leaf of the ledger, in his own good hand, and read it to her. It was a fair draft. It said that the auditor would certify to the Council that the Lower Reach had been lit for three years by a tap on the Vault's trunk line, and that House Thorne stood ready to make the line safe.

"Stands ready," Sol said. "That's Thorne's phrase. You're handing it back to him."

"It's true that they say it."

"Then say that they said it. Not that it's so." She was thinking aloud, and the thought had a shape she liked. "He asked you to vouch. Don't vouch. Report. Tell the Council what the house offered, in whose words, on what date, in front of a clerk. In that chamber, on the record, where he can't turn it into a favor he may withdraw." She heard her voice speeding and slowed it. "A man who offers can take it back. A man who has been heard to offer has only two choices left, keep it or break it, and both are public."

Kael was already writing. He stopped, read it under his breath, and looked at her over the lamp. "That's better than mine."

"It's yours with a lock on it."

"I know," he said. "That's what I mean."

They put it in plain words. *To Lord Malrik Thorne. The auditor will state to the Council what he has verified: that for three years the Lower Reach was lit by a tap on the Vault's trunk line, and that House Thorne kept the meter. He will also state that the house has offered the order for shutting the tap safely. He will not certify good faith or stewardship, which are findings he has not made. Isolde Vane joins this reply. We ask that the order be lodged under seal with the Council surveyor before the box is opened, and that it not be held against anyone's answer.*

"Witness," Kael said, and Mara set down her whetstone and came to the bench. Sol dictated her assent aloud, and Mara wrote beneath Kael's line in a square soldier's hand: *Isolde Vane assents by her word, hands unfit to sign. Mara, warden, twenty years' service, witnessing.* Sol had not asked for the last clause. She thought it was meant kindly, and found she did not mind.

The reply went out in a runner's pouch, sealed in Ashworth wax, an hour before the sixth bell. Kael watched the boy go up the gallery and did not say what he was thinking, and then did, because he had promised himself he would. "I'm afraid we've just told him no in a way he can't argue with."

"That was the idea."

"I know. I'm afraid of it anyway."

"Good," said Sol. "So am I."

Renn took the lamp and the glass to the near edge of the gallery at the change of guard, as he had said he would, and Sol went with him on the drop, with Joren on the rope behind. She could not have said why she wanted to see for herself. The gallery lay black and flat, the blue seam sleeping along its far wall. At its end the plate held the Council's chain in a long straight line, ten pins, the plumb at the tail. Renn set the glass to his eye and began to count under his breath, in tens, as he had every shift.

He reached the tenth pin. He stopped. He went back to the ninth and counted again, and she saw his lips move over one number twice.

"A hundred," he said. "A hundred, and..." He lowered the glass. His voice had gone very small. "A hundred and one."

She took the glass from him in both wrapped hands and held it badly and looked. Between the ninth pin and the tenth, where the links were dull iron and rust, lay a single link that was not. It was bright copper, untouched by the wet, and in its curve, small as a grain of rice, was a knot of three strands.

"I counted a hundred at the last change of guard," said Renn. "That was two hours ago. We were sitting forty feet above it, writing a letter."

"An ounce and a half," Joren said, who had come to look and had judged it by eye, as a man does who has carried iron. "No more. It won't move the cycle."

"It moves nothing," Renn said. "It only says that it could."

"The hatch seals are whole." Joren's voice was flat. He had checked them himself, twice, since dusk. "Council wax and ours. Nobody has come down that stair since Renn."

"Then nobody came down the stair." Sol handed back the glass. "Which means there is another way into this place."

Renn did not answer at once. He was watching the link. "And it isn't on any survey I have," he said. "And Thorne knows where it is."

Across the whole black length of the gallery, over a buried line that counted ten, the copper lay on the Council's iron, bright and unhurried.

## Chapter 20

Renn was in the chamber before them, standing where a surveyor stands when he wants nobody to ask him to move: beside the clerk's table, one hand flat on a brass case no longer than a forearm. Thorne's wax closed both ends. Across the seam ran a strip of his own, with his commission pressed into it.

"Eighth bell this morning," he said, low, before Kael could ask. "Lord Malrik brought it up the Reach on foot, with the grandson carrying nothing and looking as though he wished he were. I signed for it in front of the Chancellor's clerk. I have not opened it, and I am told I may not read it until the Council says." His eyes went to Sol's wrapped hands and then, carefully, to her face. "The link is still on the plate. Nobody wished to be the hand that changed the weight."

"The note?" Kael said.

"With the Chancellor's clerk since noon yesterday, under my seal. Joren and I walked the sublevel twice more after. No door, no drain, nothing that isn't on the survey." Renn's mouth thinned. "So the survey is wrong. I would like very much to know by whom."

Kael and Sol had settled that at the trestle, over the lamp. The note on the link would go to the Chancellor sealed and stay out of the open chamber, because a thing said aloud in front of Thorne was answered before anyone had understood it. It still felt new, to decide a thing in that order.

The chamber was full to the top tier. Eight houses sat behind their signatures, and the four who had not signed sat with the look of people who had begun to count. Lord Malrik came in on his grandson's arm, deep grey, the plain silver clasp at his throat, and the small upright woman with the copper clasp took her place behind the Thorne bench as if the spot had been kept warm. Aldous Thorne was pale. He lowered his grandfather into the seat with more care than the old man wanted, and was looked at for it.

The Chancellor began with paper, not the box.

"Before the instrument is opened, the record will hear what House Thorne filed with this office on the evening of the twenty-seventh."

The senior clerk read it standing, a bald grey man with ink to the wrist. The house had received the auditor's reply. The house had offered the order for shutting the tap safely, and did not withdraw the offer. The order, in the hand of the house's master smith, had been lodged that morning with the Council surveyor under seal, to be read by him alone until the Council decided otherwise. The house did not ask to be called a steward. It asked to be read.

Nobody on the tiers stirred. Kael had prepared for a refusal, or for a counter-offer that left the lock open at one corner. He had not prepared for a man to hand over the one page that gave him leverage, unasked, in public.

"He's given up the page," Sol said, so low the words hardly disturbed the air. "Why would he give up the page?"

"Because he has found a better thing to hold."

She turned her head and looked at him. He had not softened it, and she seemed to approve.

The box came in on two clerks' arms from the strongroom where it had been kept under guard: lead-lined oak, bound in iron, one band sprung. They set it on the table under the tall window, where the light was best. The senior clerk bent over it with a glass and took his time, and the chamber let him.

"The Council surveyor's strip is whole," he said at last. "His commission, the hour, the date. Cut words, *Twenty-three Sunspire. First bell. Unbroken.* Beside it the wax of House Thorne, whole: three strands in a knot, a drop, a feather and a flame at each terminus, near-black, in violet and copper. Two later layers lie over the first. They were laid over it and not through it, in the manner of the old trade for an instrument kept in damp." He straightened. "The Council's clerks find both seals whole."

"Enter it," said the Chancellor. Her voice was thin and carried everywhere. "Open it."

The junior clerk cut Renn's strip with a bone knife, then the Thorne wax, and lifted the lid. Inside the lead, on dry felt, lay a vellum wrapper tied with cord of violet and copper, lettered on its face: *Ward Deed of Succession*. Under it lay eleven leaves of the instrument and six more of a Schedule. At the foot of the last leaf of the instrument, in near-black wax, the knot again.

Kael had leave to come to the table. He looked at Sol first. "Read it with me."

She was already moving.

The recital was long and aggrieved: three generations of wards, the names of the dead, the cost borne. Kael let it pass. It was true, and it was there to be read past. Then the senior clerk came to the operative clauses.

"Seven. All contracts of ward service named in the Schedule, and all that follow them upon any line, pass upon ratification into the custody of the house administering reform, to be kept, assigned or dissolved at its discretion."

Kael had read a hundred clauses like it in the old ledgers. It moved a person the way a ledger moves a sack.

"Twelve. In any question touching this instrument or the contracts it governs, the reading of the house administering reform is final, and no body shall sit in review of it."

The Accord's article on transfer let a contract change hands only with its holder's consent or the sitting auditor's certification. Clause twelve turned that article into a matter for the administering house's own reading. He held the thought and let the clerk go on.

"Nineteen. This instrument takes effect upon its opening by the clerks of the Council in sight of the Council entire, its own seal being whole. If within thirty days of that opening the Council has not set it aside by the voices of nine of the twelve houses, it shall be held ratified, and the house whose seal closes it shall administer."

*Its own seal being whole.* The phrase went through him like a cold key. He knew it. It stood in the last article of the Accord's dissolution schedule, which kept alive any instrument of succession sealed under the old trade, on that one condition, so that no house should lose a lawful inheritance to the Accord's haste. The framers had written the safeguard with a decent purpose. A careful reader had gone through it before the Accord ever sat, and had drafted this to its letter.

"Sol." It came out low and fast, because he had promised himself he would say it before he had finished thinking it. "It's a seizure. Seven takes the contracts, twelve takes the last word, and nineteen makes them live. The Accord preserves any old succession deed that's opened before the Council with its seal whole. We found it. We guarded it all the way into this room, and I called that being careful."

For five days he had thought of the box as evidence. Evidence could be counted, sealed, opened before witnesses so that no one could say it had been doctored.

Sol did not answer him at once. She had her chin almost on his shoulder, reading the first leaf of the Schedule where it lay under the glass. "Nine of twelve to set it aside," she said. "Eight were enough to bring the petition. It's built to be easier to keep than to stop." She moved a wrapped finger along the margin without touching it. "Read me the heads."

He read them. Eleven houses, by line. Under the Lower Spine two names stood side by side, Thorne and Vane, as they had in her mother's contract, and a single hand had ruled a line beneath them and added them into one sum.

"Four houses on this Schedule did not sign the petition," Sol said. Her voice was perfectly even. "Ours is one. Yours is another. Nobody asked either of us."

The leaf stuck to the next. Kael's hand went out and stopped. "The corner?"

"The corner."

He held it flat with two fingers, and she turned the leaf with thumb and forefinger. It took her two tries. Neither of them said anything about it.

"Auditor," said the Chancellor. "The instrument requires your certification, if the Accord's article is to be observed. Have you a finding?"

"I have a reading, Chancellor, and it's an hour old." He straightened. "I will not certify this instrument in this chamber, on this morning's reading, with a house's petition lying beside it. I ask leave to read every leaf with Isolde Vane, under a clerk's eye, and to give a written finding at the public sitting." He drew a breath. "And I ask that the record show the first useful observation came from her. The Schedule names four houses that did not sign. Hers is one of them. That is Isolde Vane's, not mine."

"Leave is granted, and entered as she found it," the Chancellor said. She let a moment pass. "The Council also notes what clause nineteen says of the clock. It runs from the ninth bell of this morning. The Council does not stop a clock it did not set. The vote on the petition will be taken at the public sitting in the last week of Cinderveil, the last before the thirty days are spent."

Thirty days from the twenty-eighth of Sunspire ended on the twenty-eighth of Cinderveil. Kael set that beside Renn's sum for the chain, nine by the middle of Cinderveil, eight by the end, and the two clocks lay together in his head like rules on a table. Both ran out in the same week.

Malrik Thorne rose without help. "The house is content to be read, Chancellor." He turned his head toward the auditor's bench, and the dry courtesy of the turn was worse than a glare. "And obliged to the auditor for his diligence. The box could hardly have been in better hands."

"It has been in the Council's hands, my lord," Kael said, and heard how thin the answer was.

"Yes," said Malrik. "That was the hope."

Aldous Thorne had not stood with his grandfather. He was looking at the Schedule, at the Lower Spine leaf lying open under the glass, and the color had gone out of his face the way it goes out of a man who finds his own name in another man's handwriting. His lips shaped a sentence. Kael was too far off to catch more than its beginning: *I did not.*

Then the steward's fingers settled on the young man's sleeve, and the sentence ended where it was.

## Chapter 21

The reading room behind the Chancellor's dais had one window, one table and one clerk, and the clerk was the junior one who had cut the wax. His name was Bram. He had been told to watch, and he had taken the instruction so seriously that he stood against the wall with his hands folded and did not blink, like a man guarding a well.

"You may sit," Sol told him.

"I'm to stand, my lady."

"Then stand where you can see the page. You'll strain your neck."

He moved two paces. It was the most she got out of him for an hour.

The eleven leaves of the instrument lay in order along the table, each under a sheet of clear glass with a brass rim, and beside them lay Renn's fair copy, stamped in the small hours with the clerks' leave, the hour and the date on every leaf. The healer had unwound a finger's width of linen from each of Sol's hands before dawn. The fingertips had gone from red to a shiny pink. The welts across her palms were closing from the heel outward, and she could bend her fingers to the second knuckle without setting her teeth. A fist was two days off. A pen was further than that, and she did not watch Kael lay one beside the ink and then, without a word, move it to the far side of the table.

He had been up before her. His coat hung on the chair, his cuffs were rolled to the elbow, and there was ink on the heel of one hand. He slid a single sheet across to her without being asked. It bore a heading in his good hand, *Finding of the Auditor on the Instrument Opened 28 Sunspire*, and nothing under it.

"I wanted you to see the empty page," he said. "Before I put anything on it."

She looked at it for a moment. "Leave it empty until we've read it all."

"That was my plan."

"Good."

They read. Sol had been taught to read a ward-line by listening for its shape, and she found the Deed asked the same of her: where it leaned, where the weight sat. The recital leaned hardest. It named the dead of the trade, thirty-three of them in a column, by line and by year, Lower Spine and eastern line and western anchor, and at the twenty-first she stopped. A girl of fifteen. She had read that name in a Thorne ledger, in red-brown ink, four days ago, and had not thought to see it again in a legal instrument sealed before the Accord had sat.

Thorne had counted its dead with great care and set them at the front of the instrument, the way a man lays a receipt on a counter before he asks for the goods.

"It's true," Kael said quietly, watching her face. "All of it."

"I know it is." She kept her eyes on the column. "That's what I can't forgive."

He did not answer, and she was glad of it. Bram, against the wall, studied the window.

She ran her finger down the thirty-three names again, slower, looking for one that was not there. There was no Vane in the column. The dead in the recital were Thorne's dead, the ones the house had buried and inked and kept. The Vane spine had buried its own, and not one of them stood at the front of the instrument. Yet the Vane block sat in the Schedule, added into Thorne's sum.

"Their dead pay for it," she said. "Ours are in the total."

Kael's pen stopped over the margin of his notes. "That goes in the finding."

"After. When we've read the Schedule."

The Schedule was six leaves of names and numbers ruled in columns, eleven houses by line, and Sol went through it at the pace of a woman counting stairs in the dark. Kael lifted the glass for her and lowered it again. She could turn the leaves herself now, one at a time, slowly, and she did. The Vane block on the Lower Spine was as it had been in the chamber, a line ruled beneath two houses and a single sum. She looked at it until the numbers stopped being numbers, then made herself go on. Forty minutes passed that way. A bell marked the second hour, and a Council porter brought a tray with a heel of bread and a jug of cold tea, and set it by her elbow, and went out. Kael looked at the bread and then at her. He did not ask which way she would have it. She took the heel between both palms and ate, and he went back to his lens and let her.

Renn came in near the end of the hour with mud to the knee and the tally board under his arm, and stood in the door as if the room might refuse him.

"Ten," he said. "Steady on the landing at midnight and again at dawn. The chain is down an ounce and a half in six days, as my sum said it would be. The link sits on the plate beside it, and I count the two apart." His eyes strayed to the leaves under their glass and came back with an effort. "My scale is good to a quarter. I don't read law, my lady. I only wanted you to have the figures."

"Thank you, Renn," Sol said, and he went, and the door swung shut on the smell of the Lower Reach.

It was on the last leaf, under the totals, that she stopped again.

The final line of the Schedule ran the width of the page in the same square hand as everything else. It was not a name. Sol read it under her breath, and then aloud, slowly, because she had misread things this week and did not mean to do it again.

"*And all that shall follow them upon these lines, in whatever form the house administering reform shall set.*"

Kael put down his lens. "Formula. It's the common close. Any succession instrument has it, so that a replacement contract on the same line doesn't leave a gap."

"A replacement." She did not lift her eyes from the leaf. "Say it as clause seven says it. All contracts named, and all that follow them, pass into the custody of the house. Now say what follows a contract."

"Another contract on the same line."

"Written when?"

He opened his mouth and shut it again. She watched him arrive, a step behind her and then level with her, the way he had once caught up on the stair. "After ratification," he said. "In whatever form the house sets."

"It doesn't stop at the contracts we have." Her voice was steady, the one she used for measurements. "It takes the ones nobody has written yet. Thorne's petition says the old trade is dead and Thorne will draw up what replaces it. Every ward-taker who signs the new standard signs on one of these lines. After the day this goes through, every contract that gets written for the Reach is Thorne's before the ink is dry."

"The reform," said Kael. "It's the reform itself that's the seizure." He rubbed his eyes with the back of his wrist. "I read that leaf yesterday in front of the whole Council and took it for a formula."

"He asked to be read." Sol heard her own voice go dry. "I took it for a plea."

"It was an instruction," said Kael.

"The last line is a formula too. It's also the only place in the instrument that says how far 'follow' goes, and it says there is no end." She flexed her right hand slowly, a small useless habit she had picked up in a week, and the fingers came in to the second knuckle and stopped. The word she did not say to him was *first*. A person would have to be the first to sign a new standard, whoever wrote it, and a person would have to stand in front of the Reach and prove it could be survived. The thought sat in her chest at a weight she did not choose to look at yet.

Bram cleared his throat. It was a small sound, and it cost him.

"Speak," said Kael.

"My lady, Auditor. Two of the signing houses sent to the clerks' office yesterday afternoon for copies of the Schedule." He looked at the ceiling as he said it. "Both asked for their own line. Neither asked for the clauses."

Sol and Kael looked at each other over the glass.

"They want to know what they've put their names to," Sol said.

"Nine to set it aside." Kael said it flatly and did not smile. "There are four houses of us. Five of them would have to turn. Two have started reading."

"Two have started reading," Sol agreed, and allowed herself a breath. It was not much.

She turned to the clerk. "Bram. The petition's second head, the contract standard that was to replace the old form. I'd like to read it against this last line."

The young man's face tightened. "It was withdrawn, my lady."

"By whom?"

"By the petitioner. Yesterday, at noon, a Thorne runner, in order and under the house's seal. The petitioner may amend its own heads before the vote." He swallowed. "It's the petitioner's right. The clerks entered it."

Kael was very still. "Yesterday at noon. Three hours after the box was opened."

"Yes, Auditor."

"They needed the reform," Sol said slowly, "until the box was open and nineteen had started its clock. After that they only needed the lines." She looked down at the last leaf of the Schedule, at the sentence that ran off the page toward every contract not yet made. "But a hand has to have drawn it. You can't seize a form nobody's written. Whatever comes to follow these lines already exists, on a desk, in a hand, and the house has just taken it out of the record so no one can hold it up beside this page."

"Then it's in a room Thorne keeps." Kael's hand had gone to the empty sheet and stopped on it, and she watched him decide not to pick it up. "A house that keeps its archive on a stair with six retainers on it has a great many places."

"So we'll ask," Sol said. "Both of us. Before noon."

He looked at her. "Both of us," he said.

Against the wall, Bram let out a long, careful breath, as if he had been keeping the whole room upright. Beyond the window the Lower Reach went about its morning, its lamps still burning by the grace of a tap that was not lawful, and the thirty days ran on.

## Chapter 22

Ansa climbed the spire stair before first bell, which was a long way for a woman who had spent forty years bent over rope, and she would not sit until Sol had put a cup of tea into her hands, both palms round the cup, and Ansa had watched her do it. Then she took the stool by the trestle with the tar-shop's smell still in her shawl, and drew a folded paper from her sleeve, and did not give it to either of them.

"Tell me what he said to you at his door," she said. "Yesterday. I heard you went."

Kael had gone over it in the dark, the way he went over a column that would not balance. They had climbed the shallow stair at noon with a joint note asking, in six plain lines, what had become of the second head and where the standard it described might be read. Two retainers had bowed to Sol and not to him. The steward had come down four steps to meet them, small and upright, the copper clasp bright at her collar, and had taken the note without reading it. The house had said what it wished recorded, she told them. The second head was withdrawn as the Council's own rules allowed, and the house had nothing further to file. Behind her at the head of the stair Aldous Thorne had stood with his hands hanging at his sides, watching the note go into the steward's sleeve as if it were his own.

"That's the whole of it," Kael said.

"He watched the paper, not us," said Sol. "You left that out."

"I did." He had. He had not decided to leave it out; it had simply not seemed to be evidence.

Ansa nodded slowly, as if a sum had come out where she expected. "They gave you nothing because they'd already given it to us." She unfolded the paper on the trestle and smoothed it flat with the side of her hand. "The awning went up at the foot of the Lower Spine stair while you were standing at his door. Violet. Two clerks and a brazier, and tea for anyone who came to the table. Provisional engagement, they call it. For anybody who was ward-trade and has had no line since the Accord sealed. A wage from the first of Cinderveil, paid by the house, and the lamps kept." Her mouth pressed thin. "My sister's boy Tam signed at the second bell of the afternoon. Thirty-one names before they lit the lamps. I stood at my window and counted every one, because I don't read fast but I count."

Kael drew the paper toward him. Sol reached for it as well, and stopped, and he watched her decide not to be helped. She put a finger under the near corner and the sheet clung. She took it between thumb and forefinger, and it came, and neither of them said a word about that.

It was a single leaf, printed from a block, with the blanks filled by hand. *Provisional Engagement of a Ward-taker under the Standard of the House Administering Reform.* The wage. The lamps. Under them, in the same block type, a term.

"'Until dissolution of the trade,'" Sol read, "'or death of the bearer.'" Her voice had gone level, the voice she kept for measurements, and he knew what it cost. "That's my mother's term. Word for word."

"The old trade's own wording," Kael said. "Under the new name."

Below the term ran a line marked *Dependents, name and age.* Tam had filled it in careful splicer's capitals. MOTHER. SISTER, 14. Sol's finger stopped beside the second word and stayed there, and he thought of a quick forward-leaning hand on a Vane contract, *Isolde, daughter, age 8,* and did not say it. At the foot, no larger than a coin, the house's knot stood in near-black wax.

He read the rest as an auditor reads, line by line, which was the only way he had ever caught a false figure. A place for the signer's mark, another for a witness. And a last line that he read twice before he understood why it made his skin go cold: *Consent of the holder, given freely, upon the standard as set.*

"Consent," he said. "The Accord's article on transfer. A contract changes hands with the holder's consent or the auditor's certification." He laid a finger on the line. "Nobody is being taken. Every one of these is a holder giving consent in his own hand, over a cup of tea. Thirty-one of them by dusk."

"So the Deed doesn't have to seize them." Sol's voice was very quiet. "It only has to be ratified over the top of them."

"One signature at a time, and every signature lawful." He heard the audit in his own voice, the tone he used when a total came out right and was wrong. "No one will ever be able to say the house forced a single one."

"How many are there, Ansa? Ward-takers with no line."

"Four hundred, near enough. More if you count the eastern families." Ansa turned her cup a quarter turn on the trestle. "Thirty-one on the first afternoon, and the queue was still standing when I shut the shutters."

Kael did the sum and did not like it. Twenty-eight days ran from this morning to the twenty-eighth of Cinderveil. At even half that pace the house would hold the Reach's whole trade on its own paper before the Council sat, and the Council would be asked to set aside a Deed whose every contract had been signed, freely, with a witness, by the people it named.

Sol put both hands flat on the trestle. Then she closed them. It took three breaths. The fingers came in, the knuckles whitened, and the fists held. The healer had said tomorrow. She did not say that it was early, and he did not, and Ansa found the bottom of her tea very interesting.

The pen lay where he had left it, on the far side of the table. Kael did not move it toward her. She leaned and took the barrel in her whole fist, the way a mason takes a chisel, and wrote on the back of Ansa's paper, slowly, in letters the size a child makes. *Received of Ansa, one form. 30 Sunspire, first bell.* The last two letters crawled. *I.V.* She set the pen down and looked at it as if it had been a rope she had not thought she would climb.

He signed beneath her, once, in his own hand. There was nothing in it to correct.

The thought had been sitting in him since the word *awning*. He let it out before it could set.

"I want to tell you what I thought when she said thirty-one." He made himself look at her, not the paper. "I thought I could be at that table by the fourth bell in a plain coat. Sign nothing. Read the whole standard in a quarter hour and be back before you had finished with the healer. I thought it would be clean." He breathed. "I'm not going. I'm telling you because I thought it."

Sol did not answer at once. Ansa lifted her cup, discovered it was empty, and held it anyway.

"Thank you for the notice," Sol said, dry as chalk. Then, more slowly: "It would have been clean. It would also have been you alone at Thorne's table, with nobody to say afterward what you signed and what you didn't."

"Yes."

"We go at the fourth bell. Together, in the coat, so nobody has to wonder who's standing there. You ask for the standard in full, under the auditor's right, aloud, in front of the queue." A corner of her mouth moved. "If they refuse, the queue sees them refuse."

He drew a clean sheet across and wrote the request while she listened, then read it back. "*The Auditor of the Accord respectfully requests a copy of the standard.*"

"Cut 'respectfully.'" Sol did not look up. "In a queue it sounds like begging, and half of them are waiting to watch you beg. Ask for it the way you would ask a clerk for a receipt he owes you."

He struck the word and wrote it again. *The Auditor of the Accord requires a copy of the standard in full, under the Accord's article on transfer, and will read it at the table.* It sounded like a receipt. It sounded, he thought, like himself on a good day.

"Better," said Sol. "Now say it to the wall, not to me. I'm not the one you have to convince."

"They'll refuse."

"Then it will have been a very public morning."

Kael took the other half of the night out of the dark and laid it beside the first. "Renn's sealed note came back from the Chancellor's clerk at midnight. The wax whole. One line written under it: received, and the survey books of the Reach are being fetched."

"So the Chancellor's office has finally asked what the survey missed." Sol opened her fists again, slowly, and looked at her palms. "It's not an answer."

"No. It's a door beginning to move." He had not meant to make it sound like hope, and did not correct it.

Ansa got up, one hand on the trestle, and the stool scraped. She did not go to the door. She stood with her shawl held shut at her throat and looked at Kael, and he understood that the paper had not been the reason she climbed.

"One thing more," she said. "The clerk at the table. When they put the pen in a man's hand, he says it. Every one of them, the same words." She spoke them flatly, in a clerk's borrowed voice. "*The Auditor has read the standard and raised nothing.*"

The room went very still.

"I have never seen it," Kael said. "Before this morning I did not know a copy had left the house."

"I know. You've the look of a man who hasn't." Ansa drew the shawl tighter. "Tam heard it. Thirty-one of them heard it. Tam told me it's why the first ones signed. If Kael Ashworth had read it and raised nothing, it was safe."

She went out, and her slow step faded down the stair.

Kael stood with the leaf under his hand. Nothing he had done was in it. He had refused to certify and he had refused to vouch, in a chamber and in writing. Sol was watching him and did not speak. He counted the hours to the fourth bell and the names that would be on the paper by then, and asked himself how a man took back a thing he had never said, from people who had already signed on it.

## Chapter 23

The healer took the last of the linen off at first bell, unlaying it from Sol's palms the way a splicer unlays a strand, and then sat back with her hands in her lap and said nothing at all. Sol had learned that this was how the healer announced that a thing had gone well.

The skin was pink and new and shone in the window light like the inside of a shell. The welts had gone flat and white, long lines from the heel of each hand to the tip of each finger, and when she spread her fingers they opened to a count of five and no wider. She could not yet close them round anything as thick as a rope. She could close them round a pen.

"Grease at night, gloves in the wet, nothing heavier than a cup for four days," the healer said, packing her salve. "If I hear it was a knife, I'll know." She went down the spire stair without waiting to be told she was right.

Kael sat at the trestle with his back half turned, writing, and had been writing since before the healer came. He had looked at her hands once, at the moment the linen fell away, and nodded, and gone back to the page. She was glad of it. If he had said a word she would have had to decide what to do with the word.

"Read it to me," she said.

He turned in the chair. There was ink on the side of his hand and none in the margin, which meant it was the third copy. "The first was worse."

"Read me the third."

He read it in the audit voice, level, each figure set down where it belonged. *I, Kael Ashworth, Auditor of the Accord, state that I have been shown the form of provisional engagement and have not been shown the standard it refers to. I have not read it. I have raised nothing because I was given nothing to raise. I certify no engagement under the Accord's article on transfer. Let no signer take my silence for a reading.*

Sol listened with her eyes on the window, where the Lower Reach was beginning its morning below the spire and the smoke from the splicing halls went up straight. The words were good. They were also an answer to a sentence he had never said, offered to people who had already signed on it, and both facts sat in her at once.

"It needs to be heard," she said. "Paper won't reach a man who is already holding the pen. Ansa reads it at the splicers' hall at the noon break. Mara reads it on the ropewalk floor. A copy goes to the Chancellor's clerk under your wax, so it is on the record that you said it on the first day and not the tenth."

He wrote *Ansa, noon. Mara, ropewalk. Clerk.* in the margin and looked up. "Will you witness it? Your name under mine. You don't have to."

"Yes," Sol said, and held out her hand for the pen.

He laid it across her palm. She took the barrel in her whole fist, as she had on Ansa's paper, and wrote the whole of it, *Isolde Vane*, in letters the size a child makes, the last *e* sliding off the line. It took a long time. Neither of them said so. When she lifted the pen he did not reach for the sand. He waited until she nodded, and then he shook it over her name himself.

"I was wrong about the queue," she said.

"You said it would be a public morning."

"It was. That isn't what I meant." She looked at the two names, hers a child's and his an auditor's, a hand's width apart. "I told you that if they refused, the queue would see them refuse. I never thought of a house that would not bother to refuse."

Yesterday the line had stood forty long at the foot of the Lower Spine stair at the fourth bell of the morning, and it had parted for them without a word. Splicers in tarred aprons and women with children on their hips. An old man took off his cap to Kael, saw her bandages, and put it back on. Under the violet awning two clerks sat behind a trestle with a brazier at one end and a rack of tin cups, and Kael had stopped before the cloth and read out his request the way she had told him to, like a man asking for a receipt he is owed.

The younger clerk had stood and bowed to Sol first. He had ink on his cuff and a pleasant voice, and he heard the whole request with his head a little on one side, like a man being told the way to a street he lived on.

"The Auditor is welcome at the table," he said. "Here is the form of engagement. Every signer receives a copy." It was the leaf Ansa had carried up the stair. "The standard is set upon ratification by the house administering reform, as the form says, and the house cannot lay before the Auditor what is not yet set. The article on transfer speaks of a contract that changes hands. No contract has changed hands here. A new engagement is made, freely, by the holder." He dipped his pen. "The Auditor's request is recorded."

He wrote it in the table's book with the hour beside it. Then, with perfect courtesy, he poured Kael a cup of tea.

That had been the refusal. The queue had watched the Auditor of the Accord ask for a thing and be handed tea, and there was nothing in it that a man could carry home and repeat.
