"You're not saying no," he said slowly. "You're saying I don't get to decide when it's safe for you to be seen taking a title you've earned."

"Yes."

"That's harder to hear than a no."

"It's meant to be." The line at her mouth eased, not into a smile, but close to one. "You asked me to sit with it and tell you what I actually think, not what I believed I owed you for asking. This is what I actually think. I want it. I want it timed by me."

"Then it's timed by you." He said it without a beat of hesitation. "I'll draft nothing until you tell me to draft it."

"You'll want to. Before the twenty-eighth. I know you."

"I'll want to," he agreed. "That isn't the same as doing it."

She studied him, checking the sentence for the seam she expected to find and did not. "You did it," she said. "The letting-alone. You actually did it, since the bridge."

"I noticed myself wanting to ask you when I woke. I counted the mornings instead of asking."

"How many?"

"One."

"That's not nothing." She stood, and her hands, freed from their fold, would not keep still. The stair had left them cold, and she found her fingers working against each other at her belt as if she were coiling line. Kael saw it. He rose, more carefully than the leg liked, one hand on the stick, and halted short of her with the corner of the desk between them.

"You're cold," he said. "May I?"

Ask, she thought. He asked.

"Ask me slower," she said, "so I can enjoy it."

He did not smile, though his eyes did. "May I," he said, slower, "take your hands."

"Yes."

He hooked the stick over the corner of the desk. His palms were warm and dry, the pad of the left thumb stained blue with ink, and he closed both her hands inside his and held them without moving while the cold went out of her fingers a knuckle at a time. Her pulse ticked against the ball of his thumb. She had carried herself for four days like a line taken up hard on a cleat, everything in her under load and nothing let off, and now, without warning she had agreed to, the tension began to pay out. She stepped in. She put her forehead against his collarbone, and his hip found the edge of the desk and took his weight, and then one of his hands left hers and was at the back of her neck beneath the braid, the flat of it warm on her nape, not urging, only there. She breathed in ink and the cold-stone smell the cellar left in all their clothes. She stayed. He let her.

"The withdrawal stack," he said eventually, into her hair, "is going to sit there and be short whether I hold you or not."

She laughed against his collar, a small cracked sound, and stepped back before she could decide not to. "I'm going to want you to ask me things too, from here. Not defer to me out of penance. There's a difference between not deciding for me and refusing to have an opinion at all."

"Noted." He almost smiled. "What's your opinion on the withdrawal stack?"

"That it's two hundred short of meaning anything, and that you already knew that, and asked anyway to see if I'd let you off the hook for asking." She pulled her coat straight. "I won't, every time. Just most of them."

***

Ansa's boy found them at the second bell with a slate that made Sol read it twice.

The slate carried two counts now, in Ansa's blunt chalk. The top line was the standard, two hundred and fifty-two, the number it had held since Tam signed on the tenth. Under it ran a second column, the hold, and that read two hundred and eighty-eight, and beneath that, pressed harder than the rest: *Withdrawal paper signed: one.*

"One of the holders broke it," Sol said. "The standard hasn't moved. This is the other column, the holders who have held since the fourth, and one of them has put his name to the paper that ends it."

"Who?"

"The boy doesn't know. Ansa's hand, not the clerk's, so she got it secondhand at the awning and copied it fast." Sol was already turning for the stair. "Two hundred and eighty-eight held for eleven days, and now one of them has broken it in the open, where every other holder can see it done and survived."

They went down together, which had stopped being a thing either of them announced and become simply the shape of how they left a room, and found the awning crowded thin with morning trade, the brazier cold, one clerk at the table with a face like a man guarding an empty till.

"Who withdrew," Kael asked him.

"The office doesn't say names before the book's closed for the day, Auditor."

"The office will say this one, or I'll ask the Chancellor's clerk, who keeps your hours, to ask it for you."

The clerk's jaw worked. "Tam," he said finally. "Ansa's nephew, the slate boy's cousin. Came at first light, said his piece, signed, and walked out before I could send for the house. Said to tell you." The clerk stopped himself, then finished it anyway, as if the words had already left his hands once and there was no taking them back a second time. "Said to tell the Auditor: the bread's managed. Tell him thank you for the no, and tell him this one's mine to give, not his to keep."

Sol let out a breath with a laugh caught in the middle of it, thin and worn through with two weeks of not laughing. "He's telling you he doesn't need saving from this either."

"I heard it." Kael looked at the cold brazier, the one clerk, the empty stretch of morning where two hundred and eighty-seven holders had not yet followed and might, now, begin to.

"One won't move the rest," Sol said. "But a line with one strand parted is a different line. Everyone still holding it can feel where it gives."

"Then let's hope it gives the right way."

"That's the only way anyone has ever hoped for a line."

Ansa herself caught up with them before they had cleared the awning, her sleeves still rolled from the splicing bench, tar under her nails. "You'll want to know he came to me first," she said to Sol. "Before the table. Sat where you're standing and told me what he meant to do, and I told him it was his to give, not mine to keep, and then he went and did it anyway with my own words in his mouth."

"Are you angry with him?"

"I'm angry that it took eleven days and a warden hurt for one man to trust the ground under his own feet again." Ansa looked between them, sharper than her voice had let on. "You two gave him the ground. Don't let anyone tell you different, including yourselves." She turned back toward the tar-shop, then stopped. "The boy's proud of his cousin. Let him say so at you, if he tries. Don't wave it off the way you wave off everything that isn't work."

She went. The boy had trotted after them as far as the edge of the awning and waited there with his slate against his chest, the way he paused at the rope bridge when he had a thing to say and no permission to say it. Sol turned back, and did not look at Kael.

"That's my cousin," the boy said.

"I know."

"He didn't tell me first. He told my mother. Then he put on his good coat, so they'd see him properly."

Her throat had gone tight, absurdly, over a coat. "Then they saw him properly," she said. "Tell him I heard about it. And tell him I'm not going to say anything clever."

The boy considered this, and judged it acceptable, and went off up the lane at a run with the slate held out in front of him like a tray.

Bram found them on the Reach path before either of them had gone much further, a strip of Council wax in his fist and his coat buttoned wrong from a hurried morning. "The senior clerk sends this," he said, out of breath. "Commission of surveyors has moved its sitting up. Tomorrow, not the eighteenth. Dessa's asking for both of you, and for the second peg, in the room, not sent ahead."

"Why moved?" Sol asked.

"She didn't say why to me, my lady. Only when. She did say to bring the withdrawal stack, if the Auditor still has it counted."

"I have it counted," Kael said. "Tell her two hundred and eighty-seven held, one freed, by his own hand and no one else's." Bram held the strip out, and Kael took it, and read it once before he passed it to her, as he always did now, and neither of them remarked that he still did.

There was nothing on the wax but the date and Dessa's initial. Sol turned it over to be sure, and found the back as bare as the front.

"She has never once moved a sitting to be tidy," she said, when Bram had gone.

"No." Kael took the strip back and slid it inside his coat. "And she wants the peg in the room, not sent ahead. A woman who wants the object in her hand instead of in a report has already worked out what she means to do with it."

Sol looked down the path toward the ropewalk, where the roofs stood black and wet over the boiling house.

"Then what has she worked out," she said, "that she won't put in writing?"

"I don't know," Kael said, aloud, on an open path, where anyone who cared to might hear it. Nobody did. She took his free hand anyway, and they went on up the Reach.

## Chapter 38

The commission room was fuller than it had been on the fourteenth, benches dragged in from the corridor for the extra bodies, and Dessa sat at the head of it with both tally pegs laid on a square of black cloth in front of her, as if the cloth itself were evidence.

"Twins," she said again, for the benefit of the two surveyors who hadn't been in the room the first time. "Cast from the same mold. I've had a jeweler's loupe on both and I'll say it under oath if the commission wants it under oath: nobody drops a peg like this by accident, and nobody finds one by accident either. Whoever placed it wanted the Auditor's warden to find a second one exactly like the first, in the same room, days apart."

"Wanted us to know Renn had been there," Kael said. "Or wanted us to think he had."

"Or wanted the room searched again, and used the peg to make sure it would be." Dessa turned the cloth so the pegs caught the lamp. "I don't traffic in wanting, Auditor. I traffic in what's findable. What's findable is that a person with access to a caster's mold, and access to that cellar after the Council seal went on the door, chose to leave this there for your warden to find. That's a short list of people."

Sol had been quiet since they'd sat down, her coat still buttoned to the throat, and now she leaned forward. "How short?"

"Short enough that I've already sent for the caster's guild roll," Dessa said. "There are four smiths on the Reach who cast to that tolerance. I'll have names by the eighteenth."

Kael turned his head toward Sol before he went on, the way they had agreed, and she gave him the smallest nod. "I'm going to ask about the door seal," he said, for the record and for her. "Who's held a key since the fourteenth?"

"Joint custody. Council seal, Ashworth ward-band, and since yesterday a third lock the Chancellor's office insisted on, because apparently two keyholders arguing with each other was not considered sufficient security." Dessa almost smiled. "Nobody's opened it without all three present. If the second peg went in after the fourteenth, it went in with three people standing there and watching, or it didn't go in after the fourteenth at all."

"It was already down there," Sol said slowly. "Before any of us went back."

"That's my reading too." Dessa gathered both pegs into a cloth pouch with the care of a woman handling a fact that might still matter to Renn. "Which means whoever placed it did it before the water started receding, possibly before Renn went missing at all. I don't like what that suggests about the order of things, and I'd rather say that plainly than let the commission infer it wrong."

One of the older surveyors, a broad woman with a cane hooked over the bench beside her, cleared her throat. "I sat on the commission that surveyed the old ropewalk works forty years back, before the Accord, when it was still trade business and not Council business. There was talk then of a second line under the boiling house that never made it onto the plate. I was a junior hand. Nobody above me thought it worth chasing." She looked at Dessa. "I'd have said the same about the peg, thirty years ago. I don't say it now."

"Noted for the record," Dessa said, and the clerk beside her wrote it down without being asked.

The room took a moment to sit with that. One of the newer surveyors, a young man with ink still wet on his collar-notes, asked the question Kael had been holding back. "Does the commission believe the surveyor is dead?"

"The commission believes nothing it can't survey," Dessa said. "I've told the Auditor as much before, and I'll tell this room the same. Twelve days missing is twelve days. I won't dress it up past that."

***

They came out onto the ropewalk floor an hour later, the sitting adjourned to the eighteenth, the day it had first been set for, and found Corren at the cellar door with a face that meant news before he'd said a word.

"The elders sent for you," he said to Kael. "Both of you, actually, though they said it to a page addressed only to the Auditor, which I'm told is a message in itself."

"When."

"Tonight. Your father's old rooms, not the Council chamber. Small, they said. No clerk of record."

Sol's mouth tightened. "That's not how House Ashworth summons its own Auditor for a routine matter."

"No," Kael agreed. "It's how they summon him for a private one."

They walked the ropewalk floor toward the spire in silence for a while, the water sound from the cellar door fading behind them, and Kael found himself doing the thing he'd promised her he'd stopped doing: turning the evening over alone in his head, deciding what he'd say, deciding how much of it he'd say to her first. He caught himself at it on the third flight of the stair and said so aloud, because that was the other half of the promise.

"I'm doing the thing," he said. "Planning the room before I've told you there's a room to plan for."

"I noticed." Sol didn't slow her pace. "Doesn't mean I'm angry. Means you noticed it yourself before I had to point it out, which is most of what I asked for."

"They'll want to know if the intent I raised is drafted. If it's a petition already moving or a thing I said once and haven't touched since."

"Tell them the truth. It's not drafted. It won't be until I say so." She glanced at him. "You don't need my permission to tell them that. It isn't a secret between us. It's a fact they're entitled to."

"I know." He climbed a step, then another. "I keep expecting you to mind that I discuss it with them at all."

"I'd mind if you decided anything with them that you hadn't already decided with me first. Discussing isn't deciding." She reached the landing ahead of him and waited, one hand on the rail, and when he came level she did not say go. She looked at his collar instead, where the Ashworth coat had folded under on one side, and put it right with both hands, taking her time over it, the backs of her fingers warm against his jaw. "You're about to be measured by people who hold the tape," she said. "I'd like you to go in looking like the man who told Dessa the truth."

He turned his face into her palm before he had decided to, and she let him. Then she kissed him, once, and not quickly, with the stair empty above them and below, her hand at the back of his neck and his stick hooked, absurdly, over the rail. When she drew back her forehead stayed against his.

"Go. Tell them what's true. I'll be at Ansa's with the withdrawal count. Come find me after, and tell me all of it, not the parts that went well."

***

The rooms his father had kept were smaller than Kael remembered them, or he had grown since he last stood in them as anything other than a son summoned to be corrected. Two elders sat with the steward of the house, an old woman named Ferra who had signed his own appointment papers three years past, and a man he didn't know by more than reputation, a cousin from a collateral branch who handled the house's Council relations.

Ferra did not offer him a seat, which he understood as its own answer before anyone spoke.

On the writing table under the window there was a pale ring in the varnish where his father's inkstand had stood for as long as he could remember, and no inkstand. Kael found that his eyes kept going back to it. He had come braced for the elders, and had not braced for a ring in the wood.

"We've had Thorne's steward at our own door twice this week," Ferra said. "Once with condolences on your father's memory, delivered in a tone that made clear the memory was being used as a lever. Once with a private offer, which we declined without discussing terms, because House Ashworth doesn't discuss terms with the house it's meant to be auditing."

"What offer."

"It doesn't matter what offer. What matters is that Thorne believes this house is soft enough to make one." Ferra's eyes were pale and steady. "We hear you intend to petition for Lady Vane's formal co-lead standing."

"I do. Not yet. Not until the reading on the twenty-eighth has happened and been seen."

"That's a delay of your own choosing, or hers?"

"Hers," Kael said. "And mine, once she explained it to me."

The cousin spoke for the first time, his voice mild in a way that didn't match his eyes. "The house would prefer you not petition at all. A co-lead auditor from House Vane, formally recorded, is a concession the family can survive once. Survive it twice, in the wrong order, at the wrong moment, and it reads as a pattern of weakness rather than a single costly correction."

"It isn't a concession," Kael said. "It's a correction of a fact that was already true. She has done the work. The title is catching up to the work, not creating it."

"That is precisely the argument you're expected to make," Ferra said, not unkindly. "It's also the argument that will convince exactly no one who has already decided your father raised a son too tender for the seat. We are not telling you not to do it, Kael. We are telling you that the timing you've chosen, if it's truly hers and not a delay of your own making, is the only thing standing between a defensible decision and an indefensible one. Don't spend it early."

Kael looked between them, and found, for the first time in the conversation, that he had nothing to correct. "I won't."

"Good." Ferra rose, which he understood as dismissal. "Your father would have argued with you for another hour before saying the same thing. Consider yourself spared that, at least."

Kael stood looking at the pale ring in the varnish. Then he bowed, as a son and not as an Auditor, and left before either of them could see his face.

***

He found Sol at Ansa's with the slate in front of her and a second cup already poured, steam still rising, set at the place across from her before he'd come through the door.

"Two hundred and eighty-six still holding," she said without looking up. "The standard hasn't moved. One more signed the withdrawal today. Tam's is holding, and Ansa says a third man asked her, not the clerk, whether the office would think less of him for it. She told him the office had already thought less of itself for asking in the first place."

"The elders don't want me to petition early either," Kael said, sitting. "For different reasons than yours. They think it reads as weakness if it's not clearly earned and clearly timed."

"They're not wrong. They're just not right for the same reasons I am." She looked up at him then, and the tightness at her jaw had eased since the stair, the way a held breath goes out slow instead of all at once. "You told them the truth."

"I told them the truth."

"All of it? Or the parts that went well?"

Kael almost smiled. "All of it. Ferra as good as told me not to spend the delay early, which is the same thing you told me, in a voice that expected to be obeyed rather than agreed with."

"Then we agree with everyone." Sol pushed the second cup toward him. "Drink that before it's cold. You look like a man who's been standing in a room being lectured by people who used to change his linens."

"That's exactly what it was." He turned the cup a quarter turn on the table. "And the part that didn't go well. Ferra says the house has already decided my father raised a son too tender for the seat."

"Tender," Sol said. She set the slate down. "Ansa would call that a soft lay. A rope laid soft takes the strain along its whole length instead of at one point, and it outlasts the hard-laid ones because it never has a single place to fail." She said it as a fact about cordage, and then her hand came across the table and covered his where it rested on the cup, and stayed there. "Ferra meant it as a fault. I don't."

He looked at her hand over his. He said nothing, and she did not ask him to.

"The offer is the part I don't like," she said after a while. "A house refuses unheard when it already knows what's in the terms."

"Or when it's afraid of what the terms would make it want."

"Then what did Thorne put in front of them," Sol said, "that they'd rather refuse blind than read?"

Kael did not know, and said so. She left her hand where it was, and outside Ansa's window the lamps of the awning burned low over a table with two fewer names on the hold than it had carried the morning before.

## Chapter 39

Sol had meant to go straight to Ansa's from the commission steps, but the caster's guild runner caught her on the ropewalk floor instead, out of breath and pleased with himself for having found her first, and by the time she had the message straight in her own head she was climbing toward the spire rooms instead of down toward the tar-shop, because this news wanted to reach Kael before it reached the slate.

She did not knock. She had stopped knocking on his door in the last two weeks without either of them deciding it, and by now the not-knocking had become its own announcement.

He was at the window of the upper workroom with a pencil in his hand and did not hear her at first, which told her how deep in his own head he had gone. A ledger sat open on the desk with a single folded page inside its cover, and his eyes went to it and then quickly away from it when he realized she'd seen where they'd gone, the way a man's eyes go to a wound he doesn't want examined.

"The caster's guild sent word early," she said. "Dessa has four names, a day early. She wanted us to hear it from her, not from the room."

"Four names."

"Four smiths who could have cast to that tolerance. One's dead six years. One left the Reach after her husband's Reckoning went badly and hasn't been seen since. One is a boy of nineteen who apprenticed under the fourth." She stopped in the doorway, watching him receive it, and the stillness in his shoulders told her the next part was going to matter more than the rest. "The fourth cast his last commission for House Thorne, eight years back. A ceremonial die, for a seal that was retired the same year. Dessa found the commission record herself, in the guild's own book, not planted, not conveniently surfaced. It was simply there, filed the way every commission is filed."

"Eight years is a long time to hold a grudge against a die that got retired."

"It isn't the die I'm thinking about." She came further into the room, and let her eyes rest on the ledger, without asking what was folded inside its cover, because she already had a fair guess and had decided she would rather he offer it than be made to explain it. "It's that Thorne has had a hand near that cellar for longer than we assumed. Not a hand this week. A hand for years. The condolence call, the offer your elders wouldn't hear the terms of, the peg planted before the water even started to recede. None of it is a single week's work."

"You think the offer to House Ashworth is connected."

"I think Thorne doesn't spend two approaches on a house in one week by accident, and I think a house that's been declining to listen for years has finally made a patient man impatient enough to try twice." She sat on the edge of the table, not the chair, close rather than opposite, the way she'd learned suited him better when a thing had teeth in it. "I don't know what the offer was. Your elders wouldn't say and I wasn't in the room to hear it refused."

"Neither was I." He turned the pencil over once more and set it down, and she watched him choose the next sentence slowly. "They told me only that they declined without terms. I didn't press. I was too glad they'd told me at all."

"That's not nothing. A house that used to decide things and inform you after has started telling you things before you ask." She reached over and touched the corner of the ledger, not opening it, just resting two fingers on the leather, feeling him watch her fingers the way he'd watch a scale settling. "What's in here."

"A list. Four lines. The fourth one wasn't finished because it wanted to be about me and I didn't think it belonged there."

"Show me."

He hesitated long enough that she let herself notice the hesitation rather than pretend past it, and neither of them treated it as nothing.

"It isn't the petition," he said. "I haven't broken the promise. It's smaller than that. It's the shape of what I'll say when the day comes, so that when it comes I'm not standing in front of the Accord improvising the most important sentence I've ever spoken."

"That's not a violation of the timing. That's preparing to keep it." She held out her hand, palm up, and waited the way she'd learned to wait with him, patient rather than pressing. "Show me anyway. Not because I doubt you kept the promise. Because I want to see what you think the sentence is, before it's a sentence anyone else hears."

He opened the ledger and drew out the folded page and gave it to her, and she read the three finished lines twice, her thumb tracing the crease where he'd folded it, and found she was reading them slowly, because the reading had caught under her ribs.

"Her name first, not appended," she read aloud, quiet. "You wrote that about yourself in the third person."

"I wrote it before I decided it was allowed to be about you instead of about the house."

"It's a good line." She folded the page back along its own crease and handed it to him rather than keeping it, because she did not need to hold the page to believe the words on it, and she wanted him to know that. "Finish the fourth one. Just don't finish it tonight, and don't finish it because you think the house needs to hear it. Finish it when you know what you actually think, not what protects anyone."

"That might take longer than thirteen days."

"Then it takes longer than thirteen days. The twenty-eighth isn't moving. Renn isn't found by rushing a sentence that isn't ready." She stood, and felt the last of the window light on her back, gold on the roofs below and grey underneath, and thought, not for the first time, that thirteen days was a number she was starting to carry the way Kael carried his ledgers, entered and re-entered whether she wanted to or not. "I have to get back to Ansa's. The count needs finishing before the lamps."

"I'll walk with you as far as the ropewalk floor."

"You'll walk with me as far as Ansa's door, and you'll stay for the tea, because you didn't finish it last time and Ansa noticed and she'll ask after you if you don't come back and account for the cup."

"That's a serious charge."

"Ansa doesn't forgive a cold cup." She was already at the door, and paused there the way she had paused in this same doorway a hundred times since the rupture, checking without seeming to check that he was following, that the space between them still closed the same way it always had. "Bring the ledger. Not to open it. Just so it isn't sitting alone on a desk all night, being the only thing in this room that knows what's in the fourth line."

He laughed, short and real, and put the ledger under his arm, and she kept the sound of it the whole way down the spiral stair, the folded page riding against the leather cover.

Ansa's awning was down to two lamps by the time they reached it, the slate propped against the teapot, and Joren already at the near end of the bench with his coat still on, which meant he had not planned to stay long and had stayed anyway.

"Two hundred and eighty-six," he said, before either of them had sat. "Ansa's had the slate all afternoon and hasn't let me near the chalk, which is her way of saying she doesn't trust my hand near her numbers."

"I trust your hand fine," Ansa called from inside, not looking up from whatever she was doing to a pot on the hook. "I don't trust your patience for small figures. You'd round it to two hundred and ninety just to be done with it."

"I would not."

"You would." Ansa came to the doorway, wiping her hands on her apron, and set a fourth cup down without being asked, the way she always did now, as if the table had simply grown a fourth place and she was only keeping up with the furniture. "Sit. Both of you. The Auditor looks like he's been standing in a room being told things he already suspected, and that is the worst way there is to stand."

"He has," Sol said, and did not elaborate.

Joren pushed the slate an inch toward them. "Tam's holding steady. No new withdrawals signed since the one yesterday, but a woman came by at midday to ask Ansa, not the clerk, what happened to a man once his name was on that paper. Whether the house forgot him or only pretended to."

"What did you tell her," Kael asked.

"I told her the truth," Ansa said. "That I don't know, because nobody's been let go long enough for anyone to find out. That the only proof I have is Tam, still walking, still working the tar with his own two hands, not vanished, not punished, just held a little shorter on coin than he was. She didn't like that answer. Nobody likes an honest answer that isn't also a comforting one."

Sol watched Kael's face close over for a moment, private, and knew without being told where it had gone. "How is she," he asked, and did not need to say Mara's name for anyone at the table to know who he meant.

"Same," Joren said. "The healer says her color's better than it was a week back. Says it the way she'd say the weather's better than it was a week back, meaning a great deal and meaning very little, both at once." He turned his cup without drinking from it. "I sit with her most mornings before the gate. Tell her what's on the slate. She can't tell me if she hears it. I do it anyway."

"That's not nothing," Sol said.

"No." Joren set the cup down. "It's not nothing. It's just not enough either, and I've stopped pretending those are the same size."

The lamp nearest the awning's edge guttered, caught itself, and steadied. Ansa refilled Kael's cup without being asked a second time, and Sol watched him drink it before it had a chance to go cold.

"Renn," Kael said, after a while. "Thirteen days."

"Thirteen days," Ansa agreed. "I've had every boy who runs a lane between here and the old forge asking after a man with a lantern and a flat case, and not one of them has seen so much as a coat that matches. Whatever he found down there, he found it quiet, and he's stayed quiet since."

"Or he can't stay anything else," Joren said, low, and nobody answered that, because there was nothing kind to say to it and nothing true to say against it either.

Tam found them a little after, coming up the lane with the last of the tar-light behind him, his sleeves still rolled from the day's work. He didn't sit. He stood at the edge of the awning's lamp the way a man stands when he means to say one thing and leave before he's asked to say more.

"There's talk," he said, "that House Thorne's been round Ashworth doors twice this week. I don't know what for. I know a man who runs messages for the steward's house, and he says the second visit wasn't turned away as clean as the first was. Said there was a paper shown, even if it wasn't taken."

Kael sat up. "Shown, not left."

"That's what he said. Shown, and looked at longer than a man looks at a paper he means to refuse outright." Tam shrugged, the motion careful, like he already regretted having said it. "I don't put much weight on it. A messenger hears half a room and fills in the rest to make a better story. I only tell you because you would want to know Thorne was watching your own door and not just ours."

"I would," Kael said. "Thank you."

"Don't thank me yet. I haven't told you anything you can use." Tam looked at Sol, and the tightness in his face eased, the way it did whenever he spoke to her directly rather than around her. "My lady. The two hundred and eighty-six still holds. I'd tell you if it didn't."

"I know you would." Sol held his gaze a moment. "Sit, if you've a minute in you."

"I haven't. Ansa's teapot and I have never gotten along past the first cup." He was already turning back toward the lane. "Auditor. My lady."

He went, and the awning settled back into its two lamps and its quiet, and Ansa came out once more to top the pot, and Sol was fairly certain she had heard every word of what had just been said from just inside the doorway, though she said nothing about it.

Below them the Reach was settling into evening, lamps coming up one by one along the ropewalk floor, and past the farthest of them the cellar door stood under three locks. Tomorrow the caster's guild would put a name to the peg. Sol looked at Kael over the rim of her cup. He was already looking at her.

## Chapter 40

The caster's guild sat in a room that smelled of hot metal even cold, the forge banked but never fully out, and Dessa had the four names written on a single sheet before Kael and Sol arrived, which told him she already knew what the sheet would say before the guild's own master confirmed it aloud.

Dessa had come early enough to claim the seat nearest the forge, and had not moved from it since, her coat still buttoned against a cold the room had long since burned off. "Two are cleared outright," the guild master said. He was a heavy man with burn scars up both forearms, and he handled the two tally pegs on the table the way he might have handled a live coal he respected more than he liked. "The dead man cast nothing eight years and more before either peg left a mold. The woman who left the Reach was three hundred miles east by the date on the guild's own ledger when the second peg would have needed casting. That leaves two."

"The boy," Sol said.

"The boy apprenticed under the fourth name on my sheet and has never cast alone to this tolerance in his life. I'd stake the guild's word on it. That leaves one." The master set both pegs down, side by side, and did not touch them again. "Corwin. He cast the Thorne die eight years back. He's cast nothing for House Thorne since, that the guild's book shows. Whether he cast these two, I can't swear to. The tolerance is fine enough that only a handful of hands on the Reach could manage it, and his is one of them. That's as far as the metal will tell you."

Kael looked at the name written plain on Dessa's sheet, no rank, no house, a single ordinary word standing in for whatever the man had or had not done. "Where is he now."

"Working," the guild master said. "In his own shop, under his own name, same as every day this month. I sent a boy to look before you arrived. He's not run. He's not hidden. If he's guilty of anything, he doesn't know it's been noticed yet."

"Or he knows it can't be proven," Dessa said, "and he's decided the safest thing a guilty man can do is keep hammering horseshoes in plain sight." She folded the sheet once and handed it to Kael rather than keeping it herself. "The commission can question him. It cannot compel him past a question, not on this alone. A shared mold and an old commission is a thread, Auditor. It isn't a rope."

"It's the first thread with a name on it," Sol said. "That's more than we had yesterday."

"It is." Dessa gathered the two pegs back into their cloth. "I'll have him asked, gently, this week. Gently, because a man wrongly frightened talks less than a man left alone to wonder whether he should be frightened at all." She looked between them. "You have a reading to prepare for. I'd rather you spent the next ten days on that than standing over my shoulder while I ask a smith about a mold."

They left the guild-room with the name written on a strip of paper in Kael's coat, and neither of them said much on the walk back to the ropewalk floor, because there was a great deal to say and none of it belonged to a public stair.

***

Ten days passed with a fixed date at the end of them.

Kael's written finding grew and shrank and grew again on the desk in the upper workroom, the four-line list still folded inside its ledger, the fourth line still unfinished, though he had come close to it twice and stopped himself both times, not because he doubted what he thought but because he had promised the day would choose itself. The caster's name went nowhere. Corwin kept hammering horseshoes. Dessa's gentle asking, when it came, produced a man who answered every question and offered nothing beyond what was asked, which proved nothing either way and satisfied no one.

Renn did not return. On the twenty-second the search widened past Tar Lane and the boiling house to the lower forges generally, on Sol's insistence and Joren's legs, and found nothing but a fisherman on the twenty-fourth who thought he might have seen a man answering Renn's description on a skiff heading south along the coast road a full week and more past, too far gone by the time the word reached anyone to be chased. Mara's color kept improving by the healer's account and her eyes kept seeing nothing, and Joren kept sitting with her before the gate, and stopped mentioning it because there was nothing new to mention. The hold count stood at two hundred and eighty-six through the twenty-fifth, then fell to two hundred and eighty-two on the twenty-sixth, four holders signing the withdrawal within an hour of each other for reasons Ansa could not account for and did not try to invent.

On the morning of the twenty-seventh, the day before the reading, Kael finished the fourth line.

He did it alone, at the same window, with the same pencil, and did not show it to Sol that morning, not because he was hiding it but because he wanted to be certain the words would still be true by evening before he let anyone else carry them. By evening they still were. He showed her then, at Ansa's table, with Joren gone to check the gate and the awning down to its two lamps.

*Mine to answer for, whatever the reading finds.*

Sol read it twice, the way she had read the first three lines, and did not fold it back along its own crease this time. She kept it, her thumb resting against the fourth line, as if testing whether the ink had dried.

"That's not about the petition," she said.

"No. It's about tomorrow." Kael turned his cup without drinking from it, an old habit he had picked up from Joren without meaning to. "Whatever the reading finds tomorrow, about the Deed, about the tap, about whoever cast that peg, my name is on the written finding, and I intend it to stay there whether the finding is comfortable for House Ashworth or not. That's the fourth line. It isn't about you at all. It's the one I told you it wanted to be."

"I know." She handed the page back. "I didn't ask you to keep it off the list. I asked you to be honest about what it was. You have been."

"The petition still waits."

"The petition still waits. Tomorrow isn't the day for it, whatever tomorrow turns out to hold. We agreed on that a week ago and nothing since has moved the date." She said it without heat, a fact restated rather than a promise renewed. "Tomorrow is for the reading. For your finding. For whatever the Deed's thirty days decide to mean when they run out."

"Twelve houses in the chamber. The Deed stands unless nine set it aside. Eight signed Thorne's petition and four never did, so five of the eight would have to turn. Two of them started reading their own copies of the Schedule a month ago, and not one of the rest has moved."

"Then tomorrow we find out whether a written finding from the Accord's own Auditor is worth five turned houses, or whether it's worth exactly nothing to a room that's already decided." Sol looked past him, toward the dark beyond the awning where the ropewalk floor lamps burned in their long uneven row down toward the sealed hatch. "Either way, we'll know by tomorrow night. Ten days of waiting ends whether we're ready for the ending or not."

Kael folded the page along its new crease, the fourth line safely inside with the other three, and put it back in the ledger's cover where it had lived for ten days waiting to be finished. He had thought, folding it, that finishing a promise would feel lighter than this. It did not.

"Renn's wait is more than twice that," he said.

"He'd want it noted, if he were here to say it." Ansa came out to top the pot one last time before the lamps went down for the night, and neither of them said anything further about the fourth line, or the fifth house, or the reading waiting for them at the top of the Reach with the morning.

The morning of the twenty-eighth came up grey over the Reach, low cloud sitting on the spires so that the highest banners could not be seen from the ropewalk floor, only guessed at by the color bleeding faintly through the mist.

The Council chamber at the top of the Reach had not been full in Kael's memory, not for any sitting he could recall, but it was full now, benches dragged in from three adjoining rooms and still not enough, clerks standing two deep along the back wall with their tablets held ready. Twelve house seats ringed the chamber floor in their old order, each marked by a carved sigil in the stone beneath it, worn smooth by three centuries of the same twelve families sitting in the same twelve places. Eight of the twelve chairs already held their lords or their lords' delegates when Kael and Sol came through the side door reserved for Accord officers. Four sat empty, and would stay empty, by long custom, until the house they belonged to chose to fill them or chose not to. Kael had watched those four chairs empty at every sitting he could remember, and had never once heard a clerk explain why custom permitted a house to simply not answer, only that it always had, going back further than the Accord itself.

Lord Malrik Thorne sat in his own chair, upright without support, his plain silver clasp the only mark on him in a room otherwise thick with house colors. His steward stood at his shoulder, small and grey and composed, and did not look toward the side door when it opened, though Kael was certain she had heard it. She had stood at that shoulder through every sitting Kael had witnessed in three years as Auditor, through condolence calls and quiet offers and now this, and he found he could no longer picture Thorne's chair without her standing exactly there, a fixture as permanent as the stone.

Sol's own family had no chair left to sit in. House Vane's seat had gone to ash and rubble long before this sitting, and she stood instead in the officers' gallery with Kael, a place granted to Accord auditors and their attendants, one step above the floor and one step below the dais where the Chancellor's empty chair waited for the sitting to open.

"Eight chairs filled," Sol said quietly, counting the occupied chairs against what she already knew. "Four empty. Nine voices set the Deed aside, and every one of the four who never signed has to be among them, with five of the eight who did. There's no version of today where we get there on the four alone."

"None of the houses still in doubt have said which way they'll voice." Kael kept his voice as low as hers, the chamber's murmur rising around them enough to cover it. "House Estler signed the petition and then declined to attend at all. House Farrow signed it and sent a junior cousin with no authority to speak. House Vane has no chair. The fourth never signed anything, and means to wait until the reading tells it which way is safer."

"Then it comes to the finding."

"It comes to the finding." He touched the folded pages inside his coat, the written report he had carried up the Reach that morning, four lines of private promise folded inside its own separate cover behind it. "Whatever it costs the house. That was the fourth line, and I meant it before I wrote it down."

The Chancellor's clerk struck the chamber bell once, a single low note that rolled through stone and quieted even the standing crowd at the back wall. The Chancellor herself, an old woman in unadorned grey who outranked every seal in the room and wore none of her own, came through the dais door and took her chair without ceremony.

"The sitting is open," she said, her voice carrying without effort, the way a voice does when it has never once needed to be raised to be heard. "The Ward Deed's thirty days lapse at midnight tonight unless nine of twelve houses set it aside before the bell. Eight houses signed the petition. No house may lodge its voice until the Auditor of Accord has read his finding into the record." She looked toward the officers' gallery, toward Kael, and did not soften the look with anything resembling encouragement. "Auditor Ashworth. Read your finding."

Kael stepped down onto the chamber floor alone. Sol did not follow, because the finding was his to give, and she had told him so a week past, and he had agreed, and neither of them had argued about it since. He unfolded the report at the center of twelve carved sigils worn smooth by three hundred years, with the Chancellor above him and Thorne's flat grey eyes fixed on him from across the ring, and began to read.

## Chapter 41

Sol counted the chairs a second time. It was the only work the gallery left her.

Eight filled. Four not. The carved sigils under the empty ones had worn to the same soft dish as the rest, and none of the four looked more abandoned than its neighbors, which she supposed was the point of a custom that let a house stay away without ever saying why.

Kael read standing in the ring with the report held level at his chest and the stick hooked over his forearm, and in the whole first minute he did not look toward her once. She had asked for that, in her way. A man who reads to the woman he loves reads a different document from the one he reads to the Council, and she wanted the Council's.

The ferrule of the stick shifted against the stone twice as he began. She knew what it meant. The feeling in his dead leg came back as needles now, worst when he stood still, and he would stand until the last word was read before he let the weight go to the stick or to anything else.

"The finding of the Auditor of the Accord, entered at the public sitting of the twenty-eighth of Cinderveil." It was the ledger voice, dry and level, the voice that had frightened her when they met because nothing in it moved. She understood now that nothing in it moved because the weight was spread evenly. "It has three parts. I will read them in the order I found them, which is not the order that flatters me."

A clerk's pen scratched and stopped.

"First, the tap. Under the ropewalk gallery in the Lower Reach, in a sublevel the register lists as contested, the Vault's trunk line has been tapped and metered for three years. The lock plate bears the Thorne knot. For those three years House Thorne kept the Lower Reach lit at its own cost, and for those same years it kept the man who read the meter behind a locked door. I find that the light was a kindness and the lock was a crime, and that a Council which wishes to speak well of the first must be willing to speak plainly of the second."

Nothing moved on the benches. Sol watched the eight who had signed, one by one, the way she had once watched a ward for the first hairline of a fault. The lord nearest the dais studied the Chancellor's sleeve. The woman two seats from Thorne had folded her hands so hard the knuckles had gone the color of tallow. Thorne himself sat as he had sat when the bell rang, upright without support, and only the steward at his shoulder had changed, from patient to attentive. At the words *locked door* her eyes went to Thorne's face for the length of a breath, not to learn what he felt, Sol thought, but to confirm that he had let nothing show. He had not. She looked away first, which Sol had not once seen her do in a month of watching.

"The tap holds because a length of survey iron stands in for a Thorne ingot on the plate. Iron loses weight in wet air. The sum was Renn's, entered in the commission's own hand, and it brings the plate to its surge mark before the month is out." He turned a leaf. "I have not been told the chain has been replaced. The house filed its order for shutting the tap safely, in its master smith's hand, under seal, to be read by the Council surveyor alone. The Council surveyor has been missing since the first week of Cinderveil. I do not say the house arranged that. I say that the only page which shuts the tap in order can today be read by no one, and the Council should weigh that before it weighs the house's offer to keep the lamps lit."

The name went through the chamber the way a dropped tool goes through a workshop, a small hard sound and then everyone looking at the floor. Sol found Tam without meaning to. He stood at the back wall between Ansa and Bram with his sleeves still rolled from the morning's tar, and he was looking at Kael the way he had looked at the withdrawal paper before he signed it.

*First.* It was the word she had not said to Kael, or to Ansa, or to herself with the door shut. A person would have to be the first to sign the new standard, whoever finally wrote it, and stand in front of the Reach and prove it could be survived, and for weeks she had carried the certainty that the person would be her. Tam had put his name to the withdrawal paper on his own, before anyone asked, and the certainty had come loose without finding anything else to be. It sat in her chest at the same weight.

"Second, the instrument. Clause seven passes every contract named in its Schedule to the house administering reform. Clause twelve makes that house's reading of any question touching those contracts final, and bars review. The Accord's article on transfer lets a contract change hands on two conditions and no others, the holder's consent or the sitting auditor's certification. Clause twelve does not repeal the article. It hands the article to the one reader with cause to read it kindly." Kael lowered the page an inch. "I decline to certify. I find that no holder named in the Schedule was asked."

*Named in the Schedule.* Sol's mother was in it, ruled into a single sum with Thorne's own line by one hand. Her mother had signed under the old trade in a hand Sol still knew, in a room where no one asked what she would do if the ward failed, and no one had asked this time either. Kael was already reading that too, evenly, as if it were a weight and not a wound. "Under the Lower Spine the Schedule enters Thorne and Vane side by side and adds them together. House Vane has no chair in this chamber to say whether it consents. I record that the four houses which did not sign the petition were first counted in this room by Isolde Vane, on the twenty-eighth of Sunspire, and that the count is hers."

He had asked her the night before, at Ansa's table, whether he might enter her sums under her own name. She had said yes.

"I enter one thing that is mine." His voice did not change. "The instrument takes effect upon its opening before this Council with its own seal whole. It was opened here, in sight of every house, because I carried it here and guarded it and called that care. The thirty days that end at midnight began with my care. I enter that as my error, under my name, and House Ashworth may answer for it as it likes."

A breath went out in the officers' gallery behind her, let go by a clerk who had not known he was holding it. In the Ashworth chair the eldest of the elders sat with his cane across his knees and his eyes on the far wall, and behind him Ferra opened her steward's book, wrote a single line, and did not look up. On the benches, a lord of the eight lifted his head and looked at the Auditor for the first time since the reading began. Then the woman with the tallow knuckles looked at her own hands, and then at Thorne, and away. At the back wall the junior cousin House Farrow had sent, given no chair because he had been given no authority, wrote three lines on a scrap, folded it, and passed it to a page, who left by the side door at a walk that was trying not to be a run.

Sol tallied. The two houses that had sent to the clerks' office a month ago for copies of their own lines were looking at the floor. The woman made three. She needed five, and there was one part left to read.

"Third, the pegs. Two brass tally pegs, cast from one mold, were found in the cellar under the boiling house. Isolde Vane found the first in the outer chamber on the day the first ward-line was mapped. The second lay on the table in the inner room, past an iron door, after that door had been opened. The commission finds that the second was placed, and placed where any searcher would be certain to find it." He drew a breath. "The commission also finds a second ward-line under that cellar which appears on no plate this office holds. An older surveyor recalls talk of such a line forty years ago, before the Accord, when no one thought it worth chasing."

The Chancellor had not moved since the reading began. Her hands rested flat on the dais rail, and Sol thought she could see her listening in the set of the shoulders, the way a good ward-taker listens to a line before touching it.

"The caster's guild has narrowed the smiths able to cast to that tolerance to one. He is a smith who cast a ceremonial die for House Thorne eight years ago and, by the guild's book, nothing for it since. I give his name to the commission and not to this Council, because a name is a thread and I will not hand a room a rope I have not got." In the commissioners' row Dessa's mouth twitched at the word, the way a teacher's does when a pupil returns her own phrase without knowing it. Kael looked up then, finally, and the look went across the ring to the Thorne bench and stopped there. "I say only this. A house that filed its instrument to the letter of the Accord's own safeguard had, on record, the means to place a peg. The peg was placed. I do not know what it means. I know that it was meant."

He folded the report along its crease.

"That is the finding. It is signed Kael Ashworth, Auditor, and it is entered without the leave of my house."

He looked up at the gallery then, once, and she gave him a nod exactly the size she would have given a colleague who had read a difficult measurement correctly.

Sol had wanted, for perhaps ten heartbeats, to look at her hands, and did not, because the eight were still looking at him and a Council watches whoever is watched. She kept her eyes on the ring and her breathing slow and found that her left leg had gone to sleep under her.

"The finding is entered," the Chancellor said. The thin voice reached the back wall without effort. "The clerks will record the hour."

The clerk called it aloud, and Sol did the sum that everyone in the chamber was doing. From that hour to the midnight bell there was one afternoon and one evening, and five houses to turn.

Malrik Thorne rose without help. He stood a moment with his hand resting on the chair back, not for support, and when he spoke it was in the dry courtesy that Sol had learned was worse than a glare.

"The house has heard the finding, Chancellor, and will answer it when the houses are called." He turned his head toward the ring. "Auditor. I have known a good many men who could enter another man's error against him. I have not often watched one enter his own against his own house in front of every seal in the Reach. It is either very foolish or very deliberate, and I find I cannot tell which."

"Then the record will hold both readings, my lord," Kael said, "until a reader can."

The steward's mouth did not move. Her fingers did. They rose from her folded hands and reached toward the young man at Thorne's other side, the way they had reached in this same chamber a month ago, when a sentence had begun on his lips and ended under the pressure of her hand on his sleeve.

Aldous Thorne stood as they reached him.

He was still pale. Sol had last seen that face over the open Schedule a month ago, the color draining out of it as he found his own name in another man's handwriting. He stood the way he had lowered his grandfather into the chair that morning, carefully, as if the floor might object, and when the steward's hand closed on his sleeve he took it in his own and set it back in her lap. He did it gently. She looked at her hand as though it belonged to a stranger.

"Chancellor," Aldous said. His voice cracked on the title and he began again, louder. "I ask leave to speak to the finding."

The old woman on the dais turned her head. In the gallery Sol felt Kael, a single step below her, go entirely still, and understood that he had not been told, that no one had been told, and that the steward, for the first time in all Sol's watching, had nothing to do with her hands.

## Chapter 42

Kael had spent the better part of a week deciding what he would do if the chamber turned on him, and not one minute deciding what he would do if it simply stopped following the plan.

It did that now. The benches shifted a quarter turn toward the Thorne chair, the way standing grain turns toward a change in the wind, and Kael stood at the center of the twelve sigils with the folded report in his hand and understood that the finding he had built for three weeks had just stopped being the most important thing said in the room.

"Aldous Thorne." The Chancellor considered the young man the way she might have considered a leaf of the record that had worked loose in its binding. "You ask leave to speak to the finding. Speak only to what you can swear you know. The clerks will take every word, and your house will have leave to answer each of them after."

"I understand, Chancellor."

He did not look at his grandfather. Malrik Thorne had not moved from where he stood with a hand on the chair back, and his face, which Kael had watched give nothing to anyone for three years, gave nothing now with terrible completeness. Nor did Aldous look at the steward, and that was how Kael knew whose hand had been on the sleeve.

"A month ago, when the instrument was opened, I stood at the Schedule and began a sentence, and a hand I have trusted since I was small stopped it." His voice was thin and it carried, the way a wire carries when it is drawn very tight. "I would like to finish it."

The chamber waited.

"On the Lower Spine leaf, beside the Vane line, there is a witness's name. It is mine. I did not witness that line. I have never witnessed a Vane contract, or any contract in that Schedule, and no one in my house has ever asked me to. I did not write the name and I did not sign it." He stopped. His throat worked. "That is what I said a month ago in the only words I had. I do not know whose hand wrote it. I know it was not mine, and I let it stand for a month because I was told, gently and by people who love me, that an heir does not contradict his house's paper in public."

A sound began on the benches and the Chancellor lifted two fingers from the rail and it stopped.

"Clerk. The leaf, and a pen for the young man."

The senior clerk carried the Lower Spine leaf across the ring under its glass, bald head bent, ink to the wrist, and set it on the table beneath the tall window where the light was best. Aldous wrote his name on a slip of paper in front of twelve houses, slowly, like a boy set to copy a line for a tutor who was not kind. The clerk laid the slip beside the witness's name and bent over both with his lens, and the chamber let him take as long as he needed, having learned in this room a month ago what it cost to hurry him.

"The hands are not the same, Chancellor." The clerk straightened. "The young man's letters lean to the right and the tail of his last letter stays open. The witness's letters stand upright and the tail closes. It is a practised hand, and it has been careful to look younger than it is. I cannot say whose. The line carries no date."

"Enter it as found," the Chancellor said.

Kael felt the old pull rise in him, low and entire, the same shape as the stair and the pass and the coat: cross the ring, stand at the boy's shoulder, take the weight of what his own house would do to him for this. It would have looked like courage. He kept both feet where they were. Aldous, who had never once been allowed to skip a step at his grandfather's table, had just taken one nobody had set for him.

Malrik Thorne lowered himself into his chair without help. "The house will answer its grandson in its turn, Chancellor," he said, in a voice no different from the one he had used for the tap, and the steward's hands lay open in her lap as if she had put them down and forgotten where.

"It will," said the Chancellor. She looked out over the benches, all twelve seats and the four that held only stone. "The finding is entered. The leaf is under the clerk's eye. The houses will now lodge their voices in the order of the seats, beginning at the dais's right hand. A house that does not voice is not counted. The midnight bell will not wait for the slow."

The eldest of the Ashworth elders rose without his cane. Kael had known him all his life as a cough and a stick and a preference for not being asked anything, and he stood now with his empty hand resting flat on the chair back the way Malrik's had, not for support.

"House Ashworth sets the instrument aside." The voice was dry and old and clear. "It has one thing to add for the record, and our steward will read it, as she wrote it at the moment it became true."

Ferra opened her book. She read without lifting her eyes. "The house stands behind the finding of its Auditor and answers for his error as its own, the error having been made in the house's name."

That was the whole of it. Kael had prepared for a rebuke, for a silence, for a quiet cut in the accounts on a slow morning in winter. He had not prepared for the sentence to be so short. He looked at the floor stone between his boots until he trusted his face.

"House Ashworth. One," said the clerk.

The lord of the sixth chair, who had never signed the petition and never explained why, said, "Aside," and sat. It was the fastest thing anyone had done all day.

"Two."

"House Vane," the clerk read down the list, and stopped, because the sigil in the floor stone had nothing above it. "House Vane has no chair."

"House Vane has a voice," Sol said.

She had risen in the officers' gallery, and she stood with both hands at her sides and her back very straight, and Kael found that he had not known she would and that he had not needed to. The Chancellor turned her head.

"The Chancellor recognizes Isolde Vane, last of her house."

"House Vane did not sign the petition. It was not asked. Its name stands in the Schedule beside Thorne's, added into a single sum by one hand, and a witness has told this room the line beside it was written by a stranger." Her voice was the one she used for ward audits, level and without ornament. "House Vane sets the instrument aside. It would like the sum divided."

The murmur that rose this time the Chancellor allowed to run its course.

"Three," said the clerk.

Kael made himself look up at the gallery then, and she was looking back at him across the ring. She gave him no nod, no size of nod. She only lifted her chin a hair, the way she did at a ward line that had held, and sat down.

"House Lenmoor."

The heavyset lord who had asked the clerks for his own Schedule line a month ago got up slowly, like a man leaving a warm bed. He held a page in one hand that had been folded and unfolded so often the creases had gone soft. "Lenmoor put its name to a petition on the strength of a Schedule it had not read," he said. "It has read it. It finds a witness in a hand that is not the witness's, and a Vane line added into a Thorne sum, and Lenmoor does not put its name to a sum it cannot divide. Lenmoor withdraws its name from the petition and lodges its voice to set the instrument aside."

The sound the chamber made was not a murmur. It was a great many people breathing in together.

"Four," said the clerk, and his pen was not quite steady.

Kael had read of houses leaving a petition before its vote. He had never seen one do it. In the Thorne chair Malrik sat with his hands folded and his eyes on the far wall, and only the steward turned her head to watch Lenmoor sit, slowly, and set the soft page on his knee.

"House Pryce."

The woman with the tallow-white knuckles had come up a hand's breadth from her chair when her name was called, and stayed there, halfway, like a person testing ice. She looked at her own hands. She looked at Thorne. Then she looked, for a long moment, at the empty chair three places along that belonged to Estler.

"Pryce asks leave to lodge last of the seated houses."

"A house may hold its place until the seats are called through," the Chancellor said. "It may not hold it past the bell."

Lady Pryce sat. It was not a no. Kael had spent enough of his life in ledgers to know the difference between a refusal and a balance carried forward, and he found that he was counting, on the fingers of the hand not holding his stick, the way Sol did. Four voices lodged. Five more to find, and the fourth house that had never signed not yet asked.

"House Estler."

The chair stood empty. The clerk called the name twice more, at the interval custom required, and nothing came from the chair or the benches, and he made a mark in the margin and said, "Not present."

"House Farrow."

At the back wall the junior cousin who had been given no chair came away from the clerks with his hand pressed flat against his coat. He was very young, and his face had the stiff look of a man walking across ice in front of watching people. The page he had sent out at the second part of the finding had not come back through the side door, and twice while the houses were called he had glanced at it as though he expected the answer to walk through, either answer, and could not say which he wanted more.

"Chancellor." He had to begin twice. "I have no authority to voice for my house. I was told so plainly. But I was given a letter under my lord's own seal, and told to put it into the Chancellor's hand, and no one else's, when Farrow was called and not one bell before, whatever the Auditor read."

"Bring it."

He crossed the ring with his boots too loud on the stone. The senior clerk inspected the wax and pronounced it whole, and the Chancellor took the letter from the young man's fingers herself. She broke the seal with her thumbnail. She read down the first leaf, and Kael, watching from the center of the sigils, saw her face do a thing he had not seen it do in three years of sittings, in any room.

It did not last. But Kael had seen it, and so had Sol, and so had every house on the benches. Malrik Thorne, who had not once looked away from the far wall, turned his head at last and looked at her.

"House Farrow's letter will be read when House Farrow is called to answer for its absent voice," the Chancellor said, "and not before. Sit."

The cousin sat, more relieved than his face wanted to show.

"House Corvane."

The seventh chair held a thin man Kael had exchanged perhaps ten words with across the whole reading. He rose, glanced once at Malrik, and said, "Corvane holds its voice," and sat again before the clerk had finished writing it down.

Not a turn, and not a refusal. Same as Pryce, and now three of the eight signers were sitting on their hands instead of speaking, and Kael understood that the reading had not broken Thorne's petition yet.

"House Thorne."

Malrik Thorne rose. He did not look old today, though Kael knew every year of him. He set both hands on the chair back and looked once around the twelve seats, the four empty ones, the gallery, his grandson standing apart from the steward for the first time in his life.

"House Thorne will answer," he said, "when the houses are called to their close, and not before. The Auditor has read a finding. My grandson has spoken a memory he has carried a month. Neither is a verdict. I will not be hurried into one by a letter I have not read and a leaf I did not write."

He sat. The Chancellor's eyes rested on him, and then moved on.

"House Estler's absence stands. House Farrow holds a letter unread. House Pryce and House Corvane carry their balance forward. House Thorne answers at the close." She lifted her chin toward the remaining seats along the western wall. "The remaining houses will be called in turn."

Four voices lodged of the nine needed. Two more signers already wavering, and neither Thorne's own house nor its two silent allies yet spoken. Kael did the sum again, the way he had done it every night since the twenty-second, and it came out the same as always: not enough, not yet.

## Chapter 43

Sol counted from the gallery rail because counting was the only thing that kept her hands from shaking.

Four voices lodged. Five more needed, and she had learned, in the space of one long morning, that a chamber could hold its breath for hours without anyone forgetting how to breathe. House Pryce still sat with her knuckles the color of old tallow. House Corvane had not moved since the clerk wrote down his half-answer. Three seats had not yet been called, and the difference between the Deed lapsing and the Deed standing another year sat inside them.

Kael had spent the wait watching hands. Not faces, which houses trained from childhood to give away nothing, but hands, which mostly could not help themselves. Pryce's had stopped their small motion an hour ago and now lay flat and still on her knee, which he had learned in ledgers meant a decision already made and merely waiting for its moment. Corvane's still moved, a thumb worrying the seam of his sleeve, which meant the opposite.

"House Pryce."

The Chancellor had called it twice more since the balance was carried forward, each time moving down the roll and back, the custom's slow courtesy to a house still deciding. This time Lady Pryce rose before her name finished leaving the Chancellor's mouth.

"Pryce signed on the strength of a promise," she said. "That the reform would end what the old trade began. I have read the witness line myself, since the clerk's lens confirmed it. I have a granddaughter three years from her own contract." Her hands, when she lifted them from her knee, were still. "Pryce withdraws its name from the petition. Pryce sets the instrument aside."

"Five," said the clerk, and this time no one in the chamber pretended not to react. Ferra, behind the Ashworth chair, had gone very still with her book open on her knee. Kael found Sol's eyes across the ring, and neither of them moved, because five was only five, and the number that mattered was nine.

"House Corvane."

The thin lord who had held his balance an hour before rose more slowly this time, and looked, before he spoke, not at Thorne but at Pryce, who had just done the thing he had not yet found the nerve for.

"Corvane signed because Corvane was asked by a house it owed a debt to twenty years old, long since paid." He said it like a man setting down a weight he had carried further than he'd meant to. "A debt discharged does not buy a vote forever. Corvane sets the instrument aside."

"Six."

Malrik Thorne had not moved through either voice. Aldous, still standing apart from the steward, had gone the color of the vellum wrapper Sol remembered from the box at the bottom of Anchor Seven's stair. Kael had told her once, in the dark above the tar-shop, that watching a thing come apart slowly was worse than watching it break all at once. She understood the shape of that now, from the other side of the ring.

"House Estler."

The chair stood empty a third and final time, and the clerk's mark in the margin was the only sound in the room.

"House Farrow."

The junior cousin rose with the letter in both hands, no longer pressed flat against his coat but held out, as if it had grown heavier since the Chancellor had taken it from him and given it back. "My lord's letter, Chancellor. He asked that it be read aloud, not summarized, whatever it said."

The Chancellor broke nothing this time; the seal was already broken. She unfolded the single leaf and read it herself first, in silence, and then aloud, in the same thin carrying voice she had used all day.

"'To the Chancellor and the Twelve, from Lord Farrow, who could not in conscience sit among you today and watch his own signature used against a girl who buried her mother under this house's silence. I signed the Thorne petition believing the reform would end the trade that killed her. I have since learned my name was added to a witness line I never wrote, on a leaf I never saw, in a Schedule I was told did not concern my house. I do not know whose hand forges the Council's witness lines. I know it is not mine, and I do not believe it was Aldous Thorne's either. Farrow withdraws its signature from the petition. Farrow sets the instrument aside, and asks that this letter, and not my absence, stand as my house's answer.'"

The chamber did not gasp this time. It went perfectly, completely quiet, a silence Sol had heard exactly once before, at the bottom of a flooded gallery with a bell ringing far above her.

"Seven," said the clerk, and his voice cracked on the word.

"House Dellyn."

A narrow woman near the western wall rose, one of the houses Sol had never had cause to learn. She had said nothing at all through the whole long morning until now. "Dellyn has watched seven houses answer before it and heard nothing from any of them it disagrees with. Dellyn sets the instrument aside."

"Eight."

"House Tavarel."

The last seat called held an old man who took longer to rise than anyone else had all day, and the chamber, out of courtesy or out of tension, let him take it. "I have sat on this Council through three Deeds worse than this one and outlived every house that wrote them," he said. "I will not spend what's left of my patience arguing for a fourth. Tavarel sets the instrument aside."

The clerk's voice, when he called it, had stopped pretending to be steady. "Nine."

For one full breath nobody in the chamber, including the Chancellor, said a single word.

Nine of twelve. The instrument was set aside. The Deed's thirty days, which had begun the hour Kael carried a sealed box into this very chamber, would end tonight in nothing at all.

"House Ashworth's error stands," the Chancellor said into the silence, "and House Ashworth's finding stands with it. The instrument is set aside by the voice of nine of the twelve houses. The Ward Deed of Succession is void as an instrument of transfer. Its authenticity as a document is not in question. Its power to move a single ward contract from one hand to another, this day or any other, is."

She looked, for the first time all morning, directly at the Thorne chair.

"House Thorne. You reserved your answer for the close. The close has come."

Malrik Thorne rose for the last time. He did not look at Aldous. He did not look at the steward. He looked at the Chancellor, and then, briefly, at Sol in the gallery, and his face wore a look she had never once seen there before, which in a kinder man might have been called grief.

"House Thorne accepts the voice of the Twelve," he said. "It withdraws the petition it can no longer carry. It will answer, in its own time and its own house, for a signature it did not know it wore." He paused. "The tap at Anchor Seven remains Thorne's charge to close, whatever else is decided today. I will not leave that unfinished on a boy's account or my own."

"That charge is noted, and the Council surveyor's missing seal noted with it." The Chancellor set the letter down. "This sitting is closed. The instrument is void. The finding stands entered. Whatever remains between these houses, of blame, of grief, of a forged hand still unnamed, will be the Council's business on another day, and not before the bell that has, for once, not needed to ring against anyone."

The clerk did not strike the bell this time; the Chancellor simply rose, and that was apparently enough, because the benches rose with her, a great unsteady scraping of twelve chairs and three centuries of custom, and the chamber that had held its breath since first bell let it out all at once.

Sol did not move for a long moment. She watched Farrow's junior cousin sit down hard, as if his knees had only now been told the letter was delivered. She watched Aldous cross the ring, alone, and stand in front of his grandfather, and she was too far to hear what either of them said, only that Malrik put a hand on the boy's shoulder and left it there, and that Aldous did not shake it off this time, the way he had not shaken off anything all morning except the one hand that had tried to keep him seated.

She thought of Ansa, who had fed three strangers bread on a splicing-hall step and asked nothing in return but honesty. She thought of Varel, warming himself under a stolen lamp, keeping a ledger nobody had asked him to keep because he believed, eventually, the Council would need the truth of it.

Ferra caught Kael's sleeve before he reached the stair, her book still open in her hand. "The house will want a written account by the first bell of the thirtieth. Not for the Council. For the family ledger." Her voice had gone gentler than he had ever heard it from her. "Your grandfather's father lost a vote like this once. He wrote nothing down and the house has never known what he actually said in that chamber. Don't leave the same silence behind you."

"I'll write it tonight."

"Good." She closed the book. "Your elder wishes you to know he is proud, and that he will not be saying so again, so you are to consider it said for the year."

Kael almost laughed, and found that it came out steadier than he expected. "Understood."

Kael found Sol on the gallery stair.

"Nine," he said, and it was the only word either of them managed for a moment.

"Nine." She let herself lean into him, just slightly, just enough that the wardens flanking the stair would not think anything of it. "Lenmoor first. Then Pryce. I didn't think Pryce would move before the bell."

"Neither did I. I think she'd been deciding since the finding was read and needed Lenmoor to go first." He looked down at the emptying floor, at Ferra closing her book, at the eldest Ashworth elder being helped, at last, back to his cane. "My father would have called this a good day's audit and gone home to dinner. I don't know what to call it."

"Call it the first day in a month nobody died of it." Sol straightened. "The tap's still open. Renn's still missing. A hand still forged a Council witness line and we don't know whose."

"I know." Kael's jaw tightened, and then, deliberately, eased. "Tomorrow. Not tonight."

"Tonight," Sol said, "you're going to tell your house's steward that the petition you've held back for two weeks is finally allowed to be drafted. And then you're going to tell me the same thing, in words, out loud, so I don't have to read it off your face."

"Tonight," he agreed. "After I've told Joren the tally, so it isn't just the two of us who know it."

She almost laughed. It came out closer to a breath. "You're learning."

"I'm still learning." He paused at the head of the stair, and for a moment neither of them moved. "There's a version of tonight where I don't tell Joren anything until it's already decided.
